From Human Markets to Machine Markets
Feedback Stability, Auditable Memory, and Financial Constraints
Pratik Niroula
pratik.niroula@mnsu.edu
Department of Computer Information Science
Minnesota State University, Mankato
Minnesota 56001, USA
Prashant Niroula
prashantniroula20@gmail.com
Faculty of Management
Tribhuvan University
TU Road, Kirtipur, Kathmandu 44618, Nepal
September 2026
Abstract
When trading algorithms learn from prices they help set,
price formation becomes a closed loop. We ask what a
surveillance authority can learn about such feedback from
market data. In a linear market–learning model with het-
erogeneous adaptive cohorts, aggregate price responses
identify only merged rate–weight pairs, so individual co-
horts are not identified (an exact realization-theoretic
equivalence class). Separating two cohorts whose adapta-
tion rates differ byδrequires a probe budget of orderδ−4.
Any stability classifier with bounded error must abstain
near the boundaryS = 1, and with finite observation win-
dowsitmustabstainateverybudget. Localpricestability
does not imply dealer safety. The exact worst-case safe
shock radius for a leveraged dealer is set bysupt∥CJt∥,
which the Kreiss constant bounds and the spectral radius
does not. In a funded simulation with genuine feedback,
halt rates rise steeply inside the stable region because
feedback inflates the zero-frequency gain of order flow by
(1−S)−1; non-adaptive flow with the same persistence
produces the same halts. We give a size-valid test of the
completely monotone (positive-memory) class, correct-
ing a common Hankel-only criterion. On high-frequency
crypto order books (spanning a 12-day high-resolution
Coinbase baseline, an 11-day cross-venue check, and a sys-
tematic 768 asset-day multi-year panel on Binance Spot
across 2021–2024), a model-derived reduced-form esti-
mate of the flow-feedback component is consistently small
and positive (ˆQ = 0.0082, loop ratio ˆL = 0.099≪1).
It survives long-memory controls, structural SMM es-
timation (QSMM = 0.0086), and out-of-sample holdout
validation, while Rigobon heteroskedasticity reveals con-
temporaneous shock buffering (QRig =−0.01913). These
estimates are not causal and do not attribute feedback
to algorithms. All reported numbers are generated by
released code from the stated data.Keywords:adap-
tive agents; endogenous risk; financial stability; market
microstructure; performative prediction; system identifi-
cation.
JEL codes:C63, D83, G01, G14.
1 Introduction, Related Work,
and Information Architecture
1.1 Introduction
A growing share of liquidity is supplied and consumed by
algorithms that update their behavior using the prices
they help set. Price formation then contains a feedback
loop: order flow moves prices, and prices change the
order flow that adaptive strategies submit. Episodes
such as the May 2010 Flash Crash have been linked
to interactions among automated strategies [14], but
whether such feedback is present, how strong it is, and
who generates it are difficult to determine from market
data.
The question we study is:
Can a market surveillance authority detect
whether interacting algorithms are amplifying
systemic volatility using only observable trans-
action data? And if so, can public surveillance
identify which specific group of participants is
responsible?
We give partial answers, organized around four results.
1. Limits of passive identification.Cohorts that
share adaptation rates can exchange feedback alloca-
tions without changing any aggregate price response.
The identifiable object is the merged rate–weight rep-
resentation, and the minimal realization order is one
plus the number of distinct active rates (Theorem 3.4).
Detecting heterogeneity between ratesδapart needs
a probe budget of order δ−4(Theorem 3.5). Any
error-controlled classifier must abstain nearS = 1
(Theorem 3.6).
2. Price stability versus dealer safety.Because
the joint Jacobian is generally nonnormal, the exact
1

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 2 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
worst-case safe shock radius for a leveraged dealer
is γLE0/supt∥CJt∥. The Kreiss constant bounds
this radius and the spectral radius does not (Theo-
rem 3.8). In a funded simulation in whichSactually
drives prices, halts rise steeply inside the stable region.
Matched benchmarks show the mechanism is the low-
frequency content of flow, which feedback inflates by
(1−S)−2(theorem 9.2), rather than anything specific
to learning agents.
3. A testable memory class.The positive
heterogeneous-memory class is exactly the completely
monotone cone. Hankel positivity alone does not char-
acterize it. We give a size-valid finite-sample test
(Theorem 3.7).
4. What public data show.We derive a reduced-
form feedback estimand from the model and evaluate
it on high-frequency crypto books across a 12-day
Coinbase Pro baseline, an 11-day cross-venue repli-
cation, and a pre-registered 768 asset-day multi-year
panel on Binance Spot (2021–2024). We validate it
by Monte Carlo, including against spurious feedback
from order splitting. The flow-feedback component is
consistently small and positive (ˆQ = 0.0082), closed-
loop persistence is strictly subcritical (ˆS = 0.921< 1),
the loop is far from the instability boundary, and the
purely contrarian memory class is rejected. Struc-
tural minimum-distance estimation corroborates the
feedback level (QSMM = 0.0086), while Rigobon het-
eroskedasticity identifies instantaneous liquidity-shock
absorption (QRig =−0.01913; section 7).
The paper is organized as follows:
• Section 2:the closed-loop market–learning model
and its positive and signed stability boundaries (The-
orems 2.5 and 2.6);
•Section 3:identification limits (Theorems M1–M5);
• Section 4:observable identification with public
probes;
•Section 5:group identification;
• Section 6:the funded market mechanism and a
learned-agent transfer study;
•Section 7:the public-data evidence;
•Section 8:model extensions;
• Section 9:discussion, the rebuilt solvency simulation,
and conclusions.
Technical proofs, replication lineage, and research fron-
tiers are provided in Appendices A–C and the accompa-
nying Mathematical Supplement.
1.2 Related work and the precise re-
search gap
1.2.1 Adaptive expectations and endogenous fi-
nancial risk
Arthur et al.[3] model endogenous expectations in an
artificial stock market. Brock and Hommes[7] analyze
heterogeneous beliefs and routes to complex asset-price
dynamics. These are direct precedents for any claim that
agents and market conditions co-evolve. A convincing
extension must identify something not already explained
by adaptive strategy selection: for example, a measurable
feedbackoperatorthatpredictsbehavioracrossmaterially
different decision architectures.
Funding and trading conditions provide a second foun-
dation. Brunnermeier and Pedersen[8] connect market
liquidity and funding liquidity, including mutually re-
inforcing liquidity deterioration under specified condi-
tions. Consequently, losses generated by common risk
controls or liquidation pressure cannot automatically be
attributed to AI. A meaningful test must retain ordi-
nary liquidity and balance-sheet feedback in the non-AI
benchmark.
1.2.2 Algorithmic interaction and financial-
market experiments
Calvano et al.[12] show that Q-learning pricing algo-
rithms can sustain supracompetitive outcomes in a re-
peated oligopoly experiment without direct communica-
tion. This establishes that individually trained agents
can generate consequential collective behavior, although
product-market pricing is not itself a model of systemic fi-
nancial loss. Colliard et al.[19] bring learning algorithms
into securities-market pricing and identify consequences
of limited experimentation and noisy feedback. Their
mechanisms are necessary comparators for a proposed
market-making application.
Anand et al.[18] connect learning and reasoning ar-
chitectures to coordination and financial fragility in fund-
redemption experiments. Our model does not reproduce
that environment or establish transfer across their archi-
tectures. The narrower comparison is between aggregate
feedback and labeled-channel identification in a common-
rate linear model (Section 5.1). The exchange exercises
in Section 7 are separate observational diagnostics, not
validation of this equivalence for real learning algorithms.
1.2.3 Performative prediction and learning in
games
Perdomo et al.[15] formalize situations in which a de-
ployed predictor changes the distribution on which it is
subsequently evaluated. Narang et al.[16] extend the
analysis to multiple decision makers and distinguish per-
formatively stable equilibria from Nash equilibria. These
2

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 3 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
works already provide a language for prediction–action–
distribution feedback.
A recent preprint by Farina and Perdomo[17] studies
online algorithms and mixed performative stability. It is
important not to equate that learning-theoretic solution
concept with every form of financial stability. Conver-
gence of average decisions or equilibrium distributions
does not, without an additional argument, bound interim
drawdowns, market depth, collateral shortfalls, or the
solvency of a leveraged institution. Our intended bridge
concerns such financial outcomes, not a denial of existing
convergence results.
1.2.4 Dynamical systems, robustness, and mar-
ket simulation
Linearization, Lyapunov analysis, and common-metric
robustness are established mathematical tools [23, 29].
Rebranding a spectral radius is not a new theorem. Their
use here is to make assumptions, state variables, and fail-
ure modes explicit before attempting empirical measure-
ment. The challenge is particularly acute for time-varying
agents, discrete decisions, and partially observed policy
states.
For experimental infrastructure, the ABIDES paper
describes a discrete-event market simulator with exchange
interaction and configurable communication latency [30].
It is an appropriate candidate for a later implementa-
tion, not evidence that our proposed agent population
or market mechanism has already been validated. A sec-
ond implementation or an independent simulator would
help distinguish a general result from a software-specific
artifact.
1.2.5 Identification from imperfect input–output
observations
Set-membership identification already asks which models
are compatible with measured data and declared uncer-
tainty [42]. Data informativity distinguishes recovering a
unique model from learning enough to establish a prop-
erty such as stability or stabilizability [44]. Positive-
realization and steady-state-gain information have also
been incorporated into regularized identification [47, 48].
Consequently, positivity constraints, confidence sets and
gainside-informationareessentialpredecessors, notnewly
invented concepts in this paper.
Our finite-family confidence construction is deliber-
ately narrow: Gaussian independent episode vectors with
arbitrary within-episode covariance. Sign-perturbed sums
demonstrates that exact finite-sample identification meth-
ods also exist under other assumptions [49]; we have not
implemented or subsumed them. Likewise, linear-system
sample-complexity lower bounds are an established lit-
erature [45]. The new price-response two-point example
supplies a model-specific observation limit, not a new
general testing principle. The difference between noisy
hidden kernels and noisy measured prices is central to
the present integration.
1.2.6 What is newly established, and what is not
Table 1 distinguishes our theoretical and empirical contri-
butions from nearest predecessors in financial microstruc-
ture, algorithmic learning, and system identification.
1.3 Research question, scope, and claim
discipline
The operational question is:
Can changes in the joint market–learning feed-
back system identify economically meaningful
changes in disturbance amplification, beyond
what is explained by conventional liquidity,
leverage, and volatility measures?
There are three different levels of claim. Amathematical
resultfollows from an explicitly stated model. Amecha-
nism resultrequires controlled experiments demonstrat-
ing that a specified feedback channel causes an outcome.
Anexternal-validity resultrequires evidence that the
mechanism and its measurement apply beyond the exper-
imental environment. This paper supplies analytical the-
orems, a controlled group measurement-and-mechanism
study, and exploratory public-market analysis across live
cryptocurrency markets. We explicitly distinguish en-
dogenous algorithmic adaptation from static market liq-
uidity, while avoiding unverified causal claims regarding
AI specificity versus equally adaptive non-AI algorithmic
institutions.
The primary domain for the first empirical paper
should be a single market with an explicit price-formation
mechanism, inventories, and funding constraints. An
equity-like limit-order-book environment is a reasonable
experimental starting point. A fund-redemption game is
a possible alternative, but these should not be combined
into one vaguely specified “global market” simulation.
Banking, insurance, payments, and household finance
have different state variables and institutional constraints;
they are later transfer tests.
We structure the analysis around six core microstruc-
tural dimensions: machine autonomy exposure, behav-
ioral concentration, adaptation rate, leverage, liquidity,
and intervention latency. These are organizing dimen-
sions rather than sufficient statistics by assumption. A
critical surface in those coordinates may be model de-
pendent, nonmonotone, or not estimable with available
observations. Failure to find a sharp boundary is an
admissible research outcome.

2 The Closed-Loop Market–
Learning Model
2.1 A joint market–learning system
2.1.1 States, decisions, and update timing
Let xt∈Rd denote market state andθt∈Rp denote the
collection of adaptive policy states. Market state can in-
clude price deviations, inventory, funding conditions, and
lagged variables. Policy state can include learned value
estimates, forecasts, risk estimates, memory, and version
identifiers represented in an appropriate local state de-
scription. Letut∈Rk collect economically consequential
decisions. We write
ut = Π(xt,θt),(1)
xt+1 =F(x t,ut,εt+1),(2)
θt+1 =G(θt,xt,ut).(3)
The ordering is part of the model. If learning uses the
newly realized price or reward, that dependency must be
substituted into an effectiveG before differentiation. If
decisions are delayed, the necessary lags must be included
in the state. A delay cannot be appended as an informal
qualifier after calculating a delay-free Jacobian.
For local deterministic analysis, set the innovation to
zero and define
zt =

θt

Assume z∗is a fixed point and that the effective map is
continuously differentiable in a neighborhood ofz∗. This
assumption excludes unmodeled discontinuous execution
rules. For stochastic agents it may describe an explicitly
defined conditional-mean map, but mean-map stability
alone is not a claim of stochastic or almost-sure stability.
2.1.2 The complete feedback Jacobian
Applying the chain rule atz∗gives
J∗=DΦ(z ∗) =
[Fx +FuΠx FuΠθ

The upper-left block includes the market’s direct persis-
tence and instantaneous decision feedback. The upper-
right block transmits a change in learned state into mar-
ket outcomes. The lower-left block transmits a market
disturbance into policy adaptation. The lower-right block
describes policy-state persistence and action-dependent
updating.
The productFuΠx is only one channel. It is generally
not the full transition derivative. Omitting the learning
blocks can classify the frozen-policy system correctly
while classifying the actual adaptive system incorrectly.
Misclassification is possible, not inevitable: decoupled
learning or other special structures can leave the relevant
stability conclusion unchanged.

exponential memory).Suppose an algorithmic market
maker tracks an unobservable, drifting asset fundamental

If, in addition, quotes follow the linear ruleut = qˆvt
(an assumption, not a derived optimum), the induced
impulse-response kernel is
kj =qK(1−K)j, j≥0.(8)
This is a single exponential mode and hence completely
monotone. The result gives one signal-extraction ratio-
nale for exponential memory in this specific local-level
model. It does not show that real algorithms filter this
way, and other signal models (e.g. mean-reverting funda-
mentals or multiple components) induce different kernels.
Proposition 2.2(Linearized regularized gradient up-
dates yield exponential memory).Consider an auto-
mated execution algorithm adapting policy parameters
θt∈Rp via regularized Online Gradient Descent on a
strictly convex lossL(xt, Π(xt,θt))with L2 weight decay
γweight∈(0,1):
θt+1 = (1−γweight)θt−αt∇θL(xt,Π(x t,θt)).(9)
For constant learning rateα >0and stationary fixed
point(θ∗,x∗), linearization along an invariant coordinate
of the loss Hessian with eigenvalueλH > 0and cross-
derivativeℓθxyields the recursion
δθt+1 = (1−η)δθt +ηqδxt,(10)
with effective adaptation rateη=γweight+αλH, assumed
to lie in(0, 1), and gainηq=−αℓθx. The memory is
positive (q≥0) only ifℓθx≤0, which is a property of
the loss and not guaranteed.
Propositions 2.1 and 2.2 show that exponential mem-
ory arises in two simple, standard learning rules. They do
not show that deployed trading systems have this struc-
ture. The mapping is local: it requires a smooth policy,
a fixed point, and stationary learning rates. Threshold
rules, discrete retraining, nonconvex losses, replay buffers
and adaptive step sizes produce state-dependent, delayed
or switching responses that are outside the class analyzed
here. Whether a particular algorithm’s kernel lies in the
positive class is an empirical question; Theorem 3.7 gives
a test, and section 7.4 applies it to aggregate flow, not
to any identified algorithm.
2.1.3 Units and financial interpretation
Prices, inventories, and learning states have different
units. Specify a diagonal scaling matrixD using an ex
ante reference regime, then use˜J = DJD−1for norm-
based comparisons. Eigenvalues are invariant under this
change of coordinates, but Euclidean finite-horizon gains
are not. An economically meaningful output mapCy
and disturbance map Bεshould connect the state to
such outcomes as price dislocation, funding demand, or
inventory loss:
δzt+1 =J∗δzt +Bεεt+1, y t =C yδzt.(11)
The specification ofCy is necessary. Instability of an un-
observable or economically irrelevant internal coordinate
is not automatically systemic financial instability.
2.2 Local stability, finite-horizon risk,
and switching
2.2.1 A standard local criterion
Proposition 2.3(Fixed-system local stability; standard
result).Suppose the deterministic joint map in equa-
tion(4)is continuously differentiable near a fixed point
z∗. Ifρ(J∗)< 1, that fixed point is locally exponentially
asymptotically stable. Ifρ(J∗)> 1, it is unstable. The
caseρ(J∗) = 1is not resolved by this first-order criterion.
A proof of the stable case and the scope of the unstable
case are given in section A. This proposition applies an
established linearization principle; it is not a claim of a
new financial law [29].
To summarize the instantaneous spectral amplification
of the joint system, define the descriptive diagnostic
MMClocal =ρ(J∗).(12)
It is a local, model-conditioned diagnostic. Values above
or below one do not by themselves specify crisis proba-
bility, loss magnitude, or welfare. At equality, nonlinear
terms can determine the outcome:zt+1 =zt−z3
t is at-
tracting sufficiently near zero, whereaszt+1 =zt +z3
t is
repelling, although both derivatives at zero equal one.

2.2.4 Calm observations do not identify weak
feedback
Proposition 2.4(Volatility and amplification need not
move together).For the stable scalar processxt+1 =rxt+
σεt+1 with0 ≤r<1and independent, zero-mean, unit-
variance innovations, stationary variance isσ2/(1−r2)
and the response sum to a unit additive state shock (rather
than a unit standardizedεinnovation) is1/(1−r). There
exist parameter pairs with lower stationary variance but
a higher response sum.
Proof. Stationarity givesv =r2v+σ2. The response sum
is the convergent geometric series∑
h≥0rh. Choosing
(r,σ) = (0.5, 1)gives variance4 /3and response sum2;
choosing(0 .9, 0.2)gives variance4 /19and response sum
10.
This doesnotprove that unconditional tail risk rises
in the second process. For Gaussian innovations its un-
conditional tails contract with variance. Establishing
a “machine stability paradox” involving lower ordinary
volatility and higher tail losses requires a specified stress
distribution, exposure mechanism, and additional evi-
dence. The narrower result is that ordinary volatility
alone is an inadequate identifier of feedback amplification.
2.3 A tractable positive-feedback market
model
2.3.1 A price-adjustment and belief-update spe-
cialization
Let xt∈Rd be deviations of market prices from a local
reference level, andmt∈Rn be deviations of adaptive
forecasts or decision states. Consider
xt+1 =ax t +gBm t +εt+1,(17)
mt+1 = (1−η)mt +ηCxt.(18)
Here0 ≤a <1,0 < η≤1, g≥0, and B∈Rd×n
+ ,
C∈Rn×d
+ . The matrix B maps policy-state changes
into price pressure;C maps prices into target learning
states. The nonnegativity assumption imposes reinforcing
responses in the chosen coordinates. It is not a realistic
description of all financial strategies.
The market component can be interpreted as a local
reduced form of price adjustment under order imbalance.
The memory component is an exponential update, not a
derivation of optimal behavior or of a specific foundation
model. Human adaptive expectations can satisfy the
same equations. Machine-specific interpretation requires
an empirically validated link from actual agents toB, C,
g, andη.
The joint transition matrix is
J+ =
[aId gB
ηC(1−η)In
]
.(19)
A genuinely frozen-policy baseline holds the relevantm
deviations at zero and has market transitionaId. Merely
settingη= 0in the full augmented system leaves neutral
memory eigenvalues at one; that is not the same stability
claim as conditioning on a fixed policy.

Theorem 2.5(Positive-feedback boundary; model spe-

and(18), the origin of the deterministic linear joint sys-

Theorem 2.6(Signed-feedback stability boundary).Let
a∈[0, 1), η∈(0, 1]and gκ∈Rof either sign, so
that both momentum feedback (gκ >0) and contrarian
feedback (gκ <0) are allowed. The scalar closed-loop
JacobianJ=

)
is Schur stable if and only if
−1−a(1−η)

The upper inequality is the momentum boundary. It is
equivalent toS =a +gκ<1and to L =gκ/(1−a)< 1,
sinceS−1 = (1−a)(L−1). The lower inequality is an
oscillatory (flip) boundary that has no counterpart in the
positive model.
The proof of theorem 2.5 in section A.2 is a positive-
systems argument specialized to this model; the proof
of theorem 2.6 (Jury conditions) is in section A.6. The
elementary spectral result should not be presented as
an original general theorem without a literature-priority
review. Its role is to produce explicit, falsifiable conse-
quences and to check a proposed empirical measurement
strategy.

Corollary 2.7(No universal participation cutoff in this
model).Suppose, as an additional model parameteriza-
tion, g =αℓ/q, whereα∈[0, 1]is economically weighted
machine participation,ℓ>0is a specified exposure multi-
plier, andq >0is normalized depth. Ifν >0, the formal
boundary is
α∗= (1−a)q
ℓν.(24)
If α∗> 1, no crossing occurs on the feasible participation
interval. The formula is not an empirical identity and
does not establish that these factors combine multiplica-
tively in real markets.
2.3.3 Learning speed: a useful negative result
For every strictly positiveηin the assumed interval,
the boundary in equation (20) is unchanged. Update
speed changes eigenvalues and adjustment times but does
not shift this particular boundary. A claim that faster
learning necessarily lowers the instability threshold is
therefore false even within a simple reinforcing model.
Heterogeneous update rates, delays, nonlinear risk limits,
signed feedback, and endogenous depth can change that
conclusion; those extensions require fresh analysis.
For the scalar caseB= 1,C=κ≥0,

Ata = 0.4,κ= 1, the boundary isg = 0.6. Figure 2 plots
the analytic eigenvalue formula, not a fitted empirical
relationship.

3.1 Measurement and causal identifica-

3.1.1 Machine autonomy exposure without arbi-
trary category scores
Rather than assign unvalidated values such as0.1to
advisory software and0.7to execution software, define a
decision event and audit who had executable authority.
For evente, letve≥0be a predeclared exposure weight
and de∈{0, 1}indicate machine execution without a
contemporaneous discretionary human approval. For a
windowW,
MAEW =

.(26)
The denominator must be positive, and weights should
represent comparable exposure, not arbitrary numbers
of messages. Advisory use, order generation, execution
authority, and online learning should be reported sepa-
rately. Net exposure, gross executed exposure, and assets
under management answer different questions and should
not be mixed.
If authority is unobserved for some events, report
an interval: the lower bound treats unknown events as
nonautonomous and the upper bound treats them as
autonomous. This makes missingness visible rather than
assigning undocumented scores. It does not solve mea-
surement error inve.
3.1.2 Behavioral concentration is not provider
concentration
Provider concentration and common-cloud dependence
are operational quantities. Behavioral concentration in-

stead asks whether agents react along similar directions.
Let ri∈RK be a nonzero response vector for agenti on

There is an important limitation. Two equal-weight
agents with responsesr,r and two with responsesr,−r
both yield the sameS and ADC = 1. Yet their aggre-
gate signed responses arer and zero, respectively. Thus
equation (27) cannot substitute for the signed aggregate
response, price-impact direction, or the productBC. Di-
versity can remove reinforcing feedback, but it can also
remove stabilizing behavior. There is no unconditional
“more diversity is safer” theorem here.
3.1.3 An identification impossibility without
provenance
Proposition 3.1(Observational equivalence of decision–
maker labels).Consider two economies with the same
initial market-state distribution, the same market tran-
sition kernel conditional on aggregate actions, and the
same conditional law of aggregate actions given every
observable history. The economies can have different as-
signments of human and machine decision-maker labels.
They nevertheless induce the same law for all observable
market histories. No estimator using only those histories
can consistently distinguish the two label assignments or
identify different autonomy shares attached solely to those
assignments.
Proof. The initial observable distributions coincide. If
observable histories coincide in distribution throught,
the assumed conditional action law and common mar-
ket transition kernel imply the same distribution for the
next observation. Induction establishes equality of all
finite-dimensional observable distributions. Any statis-
tic of those observations therefore has the same law in
both economies; it cannot converge in probability to two
distinct label-dependent constants.
This is an application of ordinary observational equiva-
lence, not a claim that financial attribution is impossible
with richer data. Account-level decision provenance, ran-
domly assigned architecture, authorized policy-update
experiments, or credible external variation can break the
equivalence. Historical prices alone do not reveal whether
a self-exciting pattern was produced by humans, fixed
algorithms, adaptive agents, or a mixture.
3.1.4 Estimating the response system
The general operator-estimation problem below is broader
than the completed scalar experiment. Sections 4.1–4.3
provide one restricted solution using signed filled probes
and prices; they neither reconstruct every private policy
state nor identify AI participation from prices alone.
In a simulator with full state access, estimateJ by
perturbing each normalized coordinate around a speci-
fied operating point and measuring the resulting joint
next-state response. Use multiple perturbation sizes to
test local linearity and independent random streams to
quantify agent stochasticity. Finite differences of a mean
response estimate a mean-map derivative, not a pathwise
derivative of a discrete sampled action.
In observational data, fittingzt+1 =Jzt+et+1 requires
sufficient excitation, a defensible state representation,
and restrictions controlling confounding and feedback-
induced endogeneity. Hidden policy states typically re-
quire a state-space or input–output model. A reduced-
8

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 9 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
form estimate may predict outcomes without identifying
architecture-specific mechanisms; the paper must report
which claim its design supports.
An uncertainty setJ should reflect estimation uncer-
tainty and relevant model misspecification. There is no
distribution-free license to assign a narrow confidence
interval toρ(J)by ignoring dependence, near-defective
matrices, hidden states, or selected operating points. A
claim of pre-crisis warning additionally requires lead time,
false-alarm rates, and a prospectively fixed event defini-
tion.
3.2 Hidden slow learners and finite-
horizon identification limits
This section establishes the fundamental mathematical
limits of identifying latent adaptation from finite-horizon
observations. Its purpose is to distinguish a well-defined
structural stability target from the empirical information
available to estimate it under passive surveillance. The
finite-window construction, its limitations and its adverse
controls are analyzed below.

3.3 Mathematical Lineage and Mi-
crostructural Increments
Table 2 separates the classical result behind each theorem
from what is added here. In every case the mathematics
is standard. The contribution is the specialization to
the market-learning model and the resulting statements
about surveillance.
Before equal-count splitting, the witness uses
rates(1 , 1/2, 1/20)with weights(0 , 12/65, 0)and
(54/1235, 0, 240/247). Displayed decimals are rounded;
the equality is rational, not an approximate visual match.

Indeed, withpi = qiηi/k0, Jensen’s inequality forr↦→

can be false reassurance, not a stability certificate.
3.3.1 What the first measurement experiment
actually assumed
If ηi≥η> 0, r = 1−η, andTH = ∑
j<Hkj, positivity
gives
TH≤Q≤min
{ TH
1−rH, TH + r
ηkH−1

These inequalities follow directly by summing each geo-
metric component and bounding its omitted tail. They
are not optimal use of the entire moment sequence.
Under ideal loop-open impulse measurements with
known noise variance, a three-way decision rule evaluated
across 21,600 noisy measurement datasets (108 configura-
tions) achieved decision coverage of 82.69% and 66.20%
in lower- and higher-noise conditions at 256 taps without
false confident calls. However, those measurements were
loop-open impulse responses with known noise standard
error; they were not ordinary price records. Deconvolving
noisy prices does not preserve that clean noise model.
Moreover, a falsely specified learning rate floor can la-
bel an unstable model stable. These limitations demon-
strate why passive impulse measurement is insufficient
for market-wide stability certification.
3.4 Full-horizon identification audit and
fixed-order limits
We next benchmark the passive diagnostic against full-
horizon moment-based estimators that receive identical
information. This audit compares the diagnostic with
non-parametric alternatives and documents where identi-
fication breaks down under high noise. A difficult iden-
tification problem does not establish superiority of an
arbitrary ad hoc estimator.
3.4.1 A conventional full-information compari-
son
All methods received the same noisy normalized kernel
vector ˆgj, known common standard errorv, and a valid
η, wheregj = kj/(1−a). Neither generating weights,
rates, component count nor class label was supplied. A
nonnegative least-squares mixture fit used all observed
taps. A second comparator boundedG over positive mix-
tures consistent with simultaneous confidence intervals,
using continuous rate bins to protect off-grid modes. For
a featuref and masswl in binBl, its outer inequalities
were
∑
l
min
η∈Bl
f(η)wl≤Uf,
∑
l
max
η∈Bl
f(η)wl≥Lf.
This belongs to the established set-membership and
positive-measure optimization tradition [42, 43]. Stable-
model fitting alone would not be an acceptable compara-
tor for detecting instability; stability was not imposed in
these fits.
The bounds resolved 578 and 1,128 additional vectors
respectively, but did not dominate on every realization.
Some earlier decisions became model conflicts, and four
high-noise decisions were wrong. All four occurred out-
side the simultaneous confidence event. Across the 82,364
rows on that event, the reported interval contained the
truegainandmadenowrongconfidentcall. Thefullaudit
comprised 86,400 rows over four horizons. Its strongest
interpretation is a measurement requirement and bench-
mark, not an improved original summary diagnostic.

4 Observable Identification under
Public Probes: ContractI 0
4.1 An observable intervention protocol
without hidden-state access
The new research step replaces assumed kernel observa-
tions with recorded probe fills and quoted prices. It does
not identify the full latent Jacobian or the fraction of
trades performed by AI. It estimates an input–output
response and asks what that response supports aboutS
under the declared positive-memory class. This change is
compatible with established data informativity: a prop-
erty can sometimes be determined without uniquely re-
covering every parameter [44].
4.1.1 Information available to the estimator
In each ofRindependent episodes, assign one signed pulse
ur∈{−δ,+δ}, balanced across signs and randomized
independently of the initial latent states and subsequent
disturbances. Record thefilled quantity, not merely the
intended order, and the subsequent quote changesYr,j =
Pr,j+1−Pr,0, j = 0,...,M−1. Within a linear time-
invariant episode,
Yr,j =m j +urβj +εr,j, β j =κhj.(42)
The arbitrary common intercept pathmj accommodates
themeanno-probetrajectory. Theestimatorreceivesonly
(ur,Yr,·)and a valid externally declaredη. It receives
neither private states, private aggregate pressures, exact
rates, weights,a, κ, nor the disturbance variance. Initial
adaptive states need not be zero or observable: in the
experiment they are independent Gaussian draws, hidden
from the estimator.
For the exact confidence result, episodes have inde-
pendent identically distributed Gaussian residual vectors
εr ∼N(0, Σ), independent of assignment, whereΣis
unknown and may have arbitrary temporal covariance.
This is a finite-window condition; it does not require
stationary prices or closed-loop stability. Gaussian initial
states, additive flow disturbances and quote errors in the
fixed linear simulator imply it. It is not guaranteed by a
successful regression on a real market.

The covariance is estimated from public price residuals;

Theorem 4.1(Finite-family response uncertainty).Un-
der (42) and the stated independent Gaussian episode
contract, requireR >2and D >0(an even R≥4for
the implemented balanced design). Then let
z=t −1
R−2
(

)

√
ˆΣjj/D, U j = ˆβj+z
√
ˆΣjj/D.
Then P(βj∈[Lj,Uj] for allj <M|u)≥1−α. No tem-
poral independence between price observations or between
estimated response coefficients is required.
Proof. Conditional on the nonconstant design, each re-
sponse coordinate is a Gaussian regression with two fitted
coefficients. Its standardized slope error has a Student-t
distribution withR−2degrees of freedom; the residual
variance is independent of that coordinate’s slope esti-
mate. Each two-sided interval fails with probabilityα/M.
A union bound over theM coordinates yields the result
even though these statistics are dependent. In a noiseless
coordinate the interval degenerates to its exact coefficient.
This is an application of ordinary Gaussian regression
inference, not a claim to a new distribution-free method.
Exact finite-sample methods under other assumptions
already exist, including sign-perturbed sums [49].
The event covers all predeclared horizons that use
these same coefficients. It is not anytime-valid for an
unlimited search over new windows, budgets or operating
points. A 95% guarantee applies separately to a declared
measurement panel, not simultaneously to the entire
collection of thousands of simulation panels. It is also an
unconditional wrong-label bound, not a 5% conditional
error guarantee among the few cases that receive labels.
4.1.3 The four-metric evaluation standard and
conditional error rates
In partial-identification settings, an essential econometric
distinction exists between unconditional error and con-
ditional decision error. Across an evaluation suite ofN
panels, letNdec be the number of panels where the esti-
mator issues a definitive classification (stable or unstable),
and letNerr be the number of incorrect classifications.
We track four distinct metrics:
1.Decisiveness:Dec =N dec/N;
2.Coverage:Cov = 1−Nparam_uncovered/N;
3. Unconditional Error Rate:Erruncond =Nerr/N;
4. Conditional Error Rate: Errcond = Nerr/Ndec
(defined forN dec >0).
Near the boundaryS = 1, an estimator that is forced to
decide can report a moderate unconditional error while
being wrong on a large share of the calls it actually makes.
The output-error row of table 6 is an example: it issues
labels on 438 of 600 panels and is wrong on 204, so
Erruncond = 204
600 = 34.00%,Err cond = 204
438 = 46.58%.
(46)
The set-membership intervals instead abstain when their
confidence set spans the boundaryS = 1( Dec = 0%,
Erruncond = 0%), avoiding confident false calls on near-
boundary parameter profiles.

Theorem 4.2(Observable-data stability interval).Un-
der the model, a valid rate floor, and the response event
in Theorem 4.1, the trueS belongs to[S−,S +]in exact
arithmetic at every declared horizon. Declaring stable if
S+ < 1, unstable ifS−> 1, and otherwise inconclusive
therefore has probability at mostαof any wrong confident
label within the declared family.
Proof. On the response event, all trueβt lie in the clipped
nonnegative intervals. Positivity ofa, the kernel and bin
masses makes each lower convolution no greater than
its true value and each upper convolution no smaller.
Thus the true(a,w )satisfies (51). Its objective is ex-
actlya +µ([η, 1]) =S, so the LP extremal values enclose
it. Proposition 3.2 gives the label implication; the prob-
ability statement follows from the simultaneous event.
Unknown hidden states affect the observational resid-
ual distribution, not the feasibility proof, provided they
satisfy the independent episode contract.
A useful numerical anchor follows fromβ2≥ηβ0Q:

, L 0 >0.(52)
The implementation includes this redundant true-model
bound, outward feature padding, dual residual repair, pri-
mal/dual checks and a10−7classification margin. While
the exact-arithmetic LP bounds are fully verified, the
floating-point conic implementation details remain an
open numerical challenge rather than a core theoretical
contribution. Linear-programming bounds are guarded
numerically, not formally verified with interval arithmetic.
Solver failure and infeasibility are not converted into a
favorable label.
4.3 Observable-data experiments
Thefollowinganalysesevaluatetheobservable-dataexper-
imental protocol. The inputs begin with the post-probe
price, x1, and end atxH+1. Failed fits and invalid end-
points count in the attempted-panel denominator rather
than being treated as covered intervals. This ensures a
strictly conservative evaluation of the interval coverage
rates.
The corrected grid contains 600 independent panels
within 120 fixed cells. The production design crossesS∈
{.70,.82, 1, 1.03, 1.18}, slow rates.002,.02,.10, episode
budgets R = 128, 1024, probe sizes1, 4, and horizons
H = 24, 64, with five panels per cell. The aggregate rates
are descriptive averages over this fixed, heterogeneous
design, not universal performance guarantees or evidence
that every cell is adequately powered.
In the grid, 472/600 panels have a cut-limit stop at
one or both conic endpoints. A finite-cut outer candidate
is not a converged second-order-cone optimum.
Output-error inference is unavailable on 31/600 panels.
The conic routine records optimizer status, cut counts,
residuals and both objective corrections. Full outward
rounding of all assembled constraints is not established,
so certified=False is returned even when a numerical
candidate separatesS from one. The exact-arithmetic
outer-set theorem is not a proof that floating-point end-
points have been certified.

Known versus estimated covariance in the bud-
get sweep.These experiments draw Gaussian response
coefficients directly, not noisy episode histories. Their
covariance is either known exactly or estimated by an
independent Wishart draw. They are useful checks of
covariance calibration and numerical contraction, not
evidence about fills, latency or exchange data. The two
modes share coefficient-noise draws; only their covariance
treatment differs. Each production cell has 20 draws and
horizonH= 24.
Panels with a cut-limit stop at one or both endpoints
are known: 300/300; estimated: 300/300. These are
finite-cut outer approximations; the width curves do not
demonstrate convergence to the exact conic optimum.
Matched and misspecified output-error fits.
C1/C3 are single deterministic cases, not repeated inde-
pendent observations. C2/C4 use independent Gaussian
coefficient noise with known covariance, at the same
fixed horizon and noise scale. C1/C2 have one true rate;
C3/C4 have two rates but are fit by the same single-rate
estimator. This design isolates one form of structural
mismatch; it does not establish an asymptotic bias theo-
rem or identify every reason for grid undercoverage. In
particular, a profile-interval result must not be relabeled
as sandwich-interval coverage.
What the corrected studies establish.The
response-region probability calculation is a statement
under its explicit Gaussian sampling law. The compara-
tor grid evaluates implemented methods, including their
numerical failures and modeling restrictions. Neither
experiment proves estimator superiority across informa-
tion contracts, a universal impossibility theorem, or real-
market safety. The primary price-only experimental eval-
uation below serves as a separate benchmark.
Primary price-only experimental evaluation.To
evaluate observable price-response identification in full
market simulation, we analyze the primary 1,920-panel
evaluation (generated under the double-entry market
environment detailed in Section 6).
4.3.1 Response recovery and confidence calibra-
tion
The simultaneous response intervals covered all 66 true
coefficients in 1,895 of 1,920 panels, or 98.70%, versus a
nominal lower coverage of 95%. Estimated neighboring
response errors were strongly correlated: the mean abso-
lute adjacent correlation was 0.8497. This supports the
decision not to reuse an iid-kernel error model. These are
measurements from the declared synthetic experiment,
not estimates of correlations in a real exchange.
On all 5,685 horizon rows whose panel satisfied the
response event, the output-only recurrence interval con-
tained the true S. It made no wrong confident call
anywhere in the 5,760 primary rows, including the 75
rows from the 25 panels outside that event. The observed
absence of errors is not proof of a zero-error procedure.
The strongest primary design therefore resolves
16.67%, leaving 83.33% inconclusive. All 80 decisions

Across all three nested horizons, the recurrence method

The naive interval-deconvolution comparator remains
inconclusive throughout the primary panel. This is evi-
dence of looseness in that particular interval propagation,
not a theorem that all deconvolution methods fail. The
point estimator makes 1,191 wrong labels across the 5,760
rows. Its apparent 100% decisiveness is therefore not a
substitute for a supported stability statement. Nor is
this weak point comparator a basis for claiming superior-
ity over the strongest output-error or covariance-aware
conventional methods.
Importantly, statistical reporting must distinguish un-
conditional error (wrong labels divided by total evaluated
panels) from conditional error (wrong labels divided by
issued decisions,Nwrong/Ndecisive). For an estimator that
frequently abstains, a seemingly modest unconditional
error rate can conceal high conditional error among the
few decisions it actually commits to. For instance, the
output-error comparator in table 6 has 34.00% uncon-
ditional but 46.58% conditional error. By contrast, on
the primary panel in Table 11 (N = 1, 024, pulse 4),
the recurrence interval makes 0 errors among its 80 is-
sued decisions (0% conditional error, 16.67% decisiveness,
100% coverage), whereas the point fit issues 480 decisions
with 37 wrong labels (7.71% conditional error). All four
metrics—coverage, decisiveness, unconditional error, and
conditional error among issued labels—must be tracked
together to prevent misleading claims of reliability.
4.3.2 Replication budget and the cost of a useful
answer
After inspecting primary results, a separately labeled
exploratory extension uses 8,192 and 32,768 episodes,
four-share pulses and largest lag 64. Five panels per con-
figuration/budget give 60 panels at each budget. There
are 15/60 (25.00%) and 25/60 (41.67%) definite answers,
with no wrong labels. At 32,768 episodes all 20 fastest-
class panels are resolved, five moderate-rate stable large-
margin panels are resolved, and all 20 slowest-class panels
remain inconclusive. The extension contributes 2,457,600
simulated episodes but is not pooled with the frozen
primary analysis.
These results expose an observation-budget problem.
In a live market, 1,024 four-share probes would also in-
cur slippage and fees. Natural randomizations such as
tick-size pilots (section 8.4) are a possible substitute, but
none is analyzed here. Low decisiveness here also reflects
conservative bin/rectangle relaxation and discarded co-
variance geometry. They do not prove that the remaining
cases are information-theoretically impossible at these
budgets. Any methodological comparison must retain
model mismatch, unavailable intervals and the distinction
between numerical candidates and verified certificates.
4.3.3 Assumption failures are not repaired by
good price confidence
In the false-floor control, actual rates are(0.5, 10−5)but
inference is incorrectly toldη= 0.10. Weights(0.42, 0.24)
giveS = 1.06. All price-response confidence events hold,
yet the true measure is excluded from the allowed support
andall20outputsarefalselystable. Nooutput-onlyclaim
here certifies the rate floor itself.
The confounded control assigns signs using future back-
ground flow, deliberately breaking independence. The
nonlinear control replacesκVby κc0 tanh(V/c0)with
c0 = 1share. Its Jacobian at the origin retains the
linear reference, but finite pulses do not. In an exact
noise-free scaling check, initial price response per share is
0.0195935, 0.0152319 and 0.00499665 for pulses of 0.25,
1 and 4 shares. A larger pulse improves linear-model
signal-to-noise only while linearity remains valid. Passing
feasibility is not a model-validation test; failing ampli-
tude invariance can flag a problem but cannot certify the
remaining assumptions.
4.3.4 A new lower bound for the actual price-
observation family
To separate observation limits from solver conservatism,
consider a restricted subexperiment with zero initial
states, an exactly observed initial price reference, no flow
noise and independent subsequent quote noiseN(0,σ2).
This subexperiment is not the full noisy-dealer panel.
For the two models witha = 0.4, rates(0 .5,η)and

For R = 1, 024, δ= 4, σ= 0.002, H = 64, and

rates10−7,10−6,10−5, and10−4. Under the alternative
noisy-baseline level-error model(55), the corresponding
oracle errors are 49.98%, 49.76%, 47.56%, and 27.02%.
At η= 10−5an unconditional 5% wrong-label target
permits at most 21.46% balanced decision coverage under
difference errors (and at most 14.88% under level errors,
following c≤2e + 1−2p∗= 0.10 + 1−2(0.4756)) on
this pair. These are newly computedprice-lawexamples,
not the 49.64% kernel-law number in the previous audit.
The limit lets the allowable slowest rate approach zero;
it does not hold that rate above one fixed positive floor
while simultaneously sending it to zero. Each numerical
pair must be assigned a class floor no greater than its
actual slow rate. A 100,000-draw likelihood-score Monte
Carlo check accompanies the exact calculation. Neither
this limit nor the primary low decisiveness establishes a
universal crisis-prediction impossibility.
4.3.5 Accounting, numerical checks and repro-
duction
Across all primary simulated steps, maximum absolute
conservation errors were3.37×10−11dollars in cash
transfers,3.52×10−13shares and4.61×10−11dollars in
aggregate marked wealth. The largest price displacement
was about 1.010 dollars from the artificial 100-dollar
reference. These are accounting and implementation
checks, not claims of economic realism.
Twenty-two new tests pass, including off-grid feasible-
mixture checks, unknown-parameter interface tests, exact
response recovery, amplitude scaling and accounting. The
previous releases’ 29 and 23 tests were rerun and passed;
the original 625 scalar and 250 network algebra checks
were also rerun. A separately assembled group-means
regression agrees to1.95×10−16on 48 regenerated panels.
Alternative interior-point optimization agrees with the
main dual-simplex bounds to1.30×10−12across 144
comparisons. Maximum primary primal residual was
2.23×10−10; the guarded dual residual was zero to the
reported precision. These are internal cross-checks, not
independent outside review.

A second full run in a clean output directory regener-

5 Group Identification and Au-
dited Memory: ContractI1
5.1 Group identification: fundamental
limits and targeted separation
The preceding sections show how randomized aggregate
probes recover total feedbackS = a +Q from market
prices. However, evaluating systemic financial risk often
requires identifyingwhich group of adaptive agentsis re-
sponsible for shock amplification. We now establish what
aggregate price observations can identify about group
decomposition, and prove an exact non-identifiability
result.
5.1.1 The swapped-feedback non-identifiability
theorem
Consider two economies with two adaptive agents sharing
a common learning rateη∈(0, 1], price persistencea∈
[0,1), and impactκ>0, initialized at zero:
xt+1 =ax t +
2∑
i=1
yi,t +κut +et+1,(56)
yi,t+1 = (1−η)yi,t +ηqixt.(57)
Let Economy A have feedback weights( q1,q 2) =
(0.30,0.10)and Economy B have(q1,q 2) = (0.10,0.30).
Proposition 5.1(Swapped-feedback non-identifiabil-
ity).Both economies induce identical aggregate price
impulse responses at all horizons. If aggregate distur-
bances also have the same joint law in both economies, no
estimator observing only prices and market-wide probes
can distinguish them. Identical deterministic responses
alone do not imply this stochastic claim when distur-
bance laws differ. Nevertheless, for a policy interven-
tion that suppresses Agent 1 (settingq1 = 0), the feed-
back reduction is∆Q1 = q1 = 0.30in Economy A and
∆Q1 = 0.10in Economy B. Defining the normalized loop
gain asG =Q/(1−a)(distinguished from the steady-state
price response to constant input,κ/(1−a−Q)), holding
a = 0.40fixed the corresponding reduction in normalized
loop gain is∆ G1 = q1/(1−a) = 0.50in Economy A
versus∆ G1≈0.167in Economy B, differing by a factor
of three.
Proof. Summing the memory equations gives Yt =∑
iyi,t, satisfyingYt+1 = (1−η)Yt +η(q1 +q2)xt. The
closed-loop price dynamics depend on the parameter vec-
tor q solely through the scalar sumQ =q1 +q2. Since
Q = 0.40inbotheconomies, the rational transferfunction
fromu tox is identical at all horizons; see Section A.5 for
the full resolvent matrix expansion. In state space, this
corresponds to an exact Popov-Belevitch-Hautus (PBH)
unobservable eigenspace defect of dimensionn−1at
eigenvalueλ= 1−η, meaning any parameter variation
along ∑∆qi = 0produces zero observable deflection.
Consequently, the likelihood ratio of any aggregate price
sequence under the two models is identically one. Yet
counterfactual suppression removesq1, which differs by
0.20.
Hence aggregate prices carry no information about
group attribution in the common-rate symmetric bench-
mark, even with infinite price records and zero measure-
ment noise.
5.1.2 Minimal separation in the common-rate
observationally symmetric model

common learning rateηi =η∈(0, 1]. Aggregate price im-

allocation uniformly over the nonnegative parameter class.
This does not rule out degenerate pointwise cases such as
a known zero total. When channel probing experiments
are conducted according to anm×nexperimental mea-
surement matrixU, the measured lag-2 response vector
satisfies the linear measurement map:

Remark5.3 (Identification under heterogeneous dynam-
ics).When agent cohorts possess known, distinct learning
rates η1̸=η2̸=...̸=ηn, the aggregate impulse kernel
kj = ∑n
i=1qiηi(1−ηi)j comprises distinct exponential
modes. In that asymmetric setting, observing n dis-
tinct impulse moments can permit Vandermonde-type
separation of(q1,...,qn)from aggregate prices without
channel-specific probing, provided the modes(1 −ηi)
are sufficiently separated. Theorem 5.2 characterizes the
minimal separation requirements specifically in the obser-
vationally symmetric benchmark where temporal mode
degeneracy prevents passive separation.
5.2 Auditable memory tails: what a
source contract can justify
Passive or unconstrained price observation experiments
leave a consequential gap: a short response record can-
not validate the minimum learning rate that makes an
asymptotic upper bound finite without structural side-
information. We therefore separate animplementation-
conditioned tail claim, anobservable linear-gain claim,
and afinite-horizon operational-risk claim. They have dis-
tinct assumptions, targets, and information requirements.
A successful result for one is not silently promoted to the
others. In this section, we examine what an auditable
execution contract can and cannot justify.
5.2.1 A complete channel inventory, not a
guessed rate floor
The audited experimental class has the public update
rule
mi,t+1 = (1−ηi)mi,t +ηixt,
xt+1 =ax t +
∑
i
qimi,t +λut +wt+1,(59)
with mi,0 = x0 = 0,0 ≤a <1, qi≥0, ∑
iqi≤Qmax,
and publicηi∈[η0, 1]. Here Qmax = 1is enforced by
input validation in the released linear source. The rates,
reset, update timing, and absence of other feedback chan-
nels are architectural disclosures; they are not estimated
from price records. The actualqi, a, andλare withheld
from the estimator.
The release includes a machine-readable contract with
source digest, public rates, zero-reset declaration, obser-
vation specification and allowed execution regime. Unit
22

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 23 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
tests reject declared changes to the reset, source digest,
observation type, or execution regime. In the experiment
the contract is issued from the actual public simulation
configuration, and the source is available for inspection.
This justifies the restrictionwithin that controlled imple-
mentation, not in an arbitrary proprietary market.
A digest establishes file identity, not completeness,
truthful reporting, or secure runtime attestation. A de-
liberately self-issued contract containing false rates still
passes a syntax-and-digest check. This failure is included
in the release. An outside application needs authenticated
configuration and an independently inspected inventory
of every feedback-relevant channel. A manifest alone is
not a safety certificate. In particular, a signal-memory
reset does not reset account equity, outstanding positions,
leverage rules, or other agents.

For Qmax = 1and H = 64, the envelopes atη0 =

large unseen tail. A verified small rate is not equivalent
to a useful small tail. If an additional relevant inventory
coordinate has unit persistence, the premiser <1for
the complete hidden subsystem is unavailable; a contract
for the memory module cannot fill that gap.
5.2.2 Why the earlier impossibility results still
matter
The exact and noisy counterexamples in Sections 3.2–3.4
are not contradicted. They concern a less informative
observation class. Disclosing the true update architecture
and guaranteeing a reset changes the compatible model
set. That disclosure has an institutional cost and is not
available merely because prices are observed accurately.
The distinction between information sufficient for a prop-
erty and information sufficient for full model recovery is
established in data-informativity research [44]; positive
side-information is likewise an existing identification tool
[47]. Our contribution here is an explicit, executable
conditional contract and a documented failure when its
scope is misrepresented.
5.3 Budgeted inference from audited
public dynamics
5.3.1 One terminal innovation per independent
episode
Given public rates, exact midprice deviations and a veri-
fied reset, the estimator reconstructs

It is not selected after a favorable price movement.
Proposition 5.5(Conditional terminal-row confidence;
classical regression).Suppose episodes are independent,
the innovationswj,Tj are independent Gaussian variables
with common unknown variance, and each is independent
of all pre-terminal regressors and preassigned endpoints.
If X has full column rankp<n , then for a fixed contrast

Unknown-rate recurrence bounds and the present struc-
tural estimator do not receive the same information: the
former operate under sensor noise without channel dis-
closure, whereas the latter utilizes disclosed architectural
rates and exact midquote updates. Consequently, dif-
ferences in decisiveness reflect differences in information
structure rather than raw statistical efficiency alone.
5.3.2 Primary budget experiment and negative
results
The primary identification panel investigates
twelve benchmark( G,ηslow)combinations: G∈
{0.7, 0.95, 1.05, 1.3}, ηslow ∈ {0.002, 0.02, 0.1}, a fast
rate of0.5, a = 0.4, λ= 0.02, andq = 0.6G(0.6, 0.4).
Gaussian innovation standard deviation is0.002dollars.
Gains and innovations are not inputs to inference. There
are n∈ {32, 128, 512}independent episodes, lengths
T∈{16, 64}, two probe designs, and twenty replicate
panels per cell: 2,880 panels and 645,120 episode
trajectories.
The two designs have four shares of within-window
gross probe volume: an initial signed four-share impulse
or eight half-share orders in four round trips. They do
nothave equal squared input energy or residual inventory.
The impulse has energy 16 share-squared and holds four
shares at its endpoint; the round trips have energy 2
and return flat, with peak absolute inventory at most
half a share. Unwinding the impulse after observation
requires another four shares of volume. These economic
differences are reported rather than hidden behind a
claim of “equal budget.” Input design under amplitude
and power constraints is established research [52]; neither
pattern is claimed optimal.
Across primary panels, 2,758 of 2,880 gain intervals
cover the true gain, establishing a parameter coverage
of 95.76%. Of the 2,880 panels, 745 produce definite
classification decisions (a decisiveness rate of 25.87%),
while 2,135 abstain. Among all 2,880 panels, twenty
result in confident errors (an unconditional error rate
of 0.69%). Crucially, evaluated among the 745 issued
decisions, the conditional error rate is20/745 = 2.68%.
Therefore, this audited protocol does not eliminate con-
ditional error: while abstention correctly filters out most
ambiguous windows near boundaryS = 1, issued deci-
sions still retain a non-zero error rate of 2.68%. These are
empirical frequencies under per-panel 95% intervals, not
simultaneous study-wide coverage. AtT = 64,n = 128,
the impulse resolves 86/240 panels, versus 46/240 for
the round trips. Atn = 512, those counts are 158/240
and 80/240. The round-trip alternative fails as a general
accuracy improvement. Its smaller position exposure is
a separate property, not proof of equivalent informative-
ness.
5.3.3 Held-out endpoint diversification: useful
at small budgets, not uniformly
After the primary experiment, a small pilot suggested
varying the observation endpoint instead of redistributing
the order volume. A fresh-seed follow-up was specified
before its execution. Both arms use the same initial
four-share impulse and the same audited rates. One arm
records 64 transitions per episode. The other assigns
equal numbers of episodes to 8, 16, 32 and 64 transitions,
shuffled before inspecting prices. Its average record is
30 transitions, with the same maximum of 64. Both
arms would require the same four-share post-observation
unwind, outside the measurement law.
Each paired dataset is simulated to length 64, but the
diversified estimator is given only the assigned prefixes; a
test alters all later prices and verifies unchanged inference.
There are 720 fresh panel datasets (161,280 episodes),
each evaluated in both arms, yielding 1,440 decision rows.
This is post-primary held-out confirmation, not external
preregistration. The contrasts, model grid and new seeds
are released.
At 32 episodes, 56 previously unresolved paired cases
become definite and seven go the opposite way: 49 net
additional decisions. At 128, the corresponding counts
are 42 and sixteen. At 512, diversification loses fifteen de-
cisions net. Thus the result is a budget-dependent trade-
off, not a uniformly dominating detector. The release
includes descriptive paired tests; pooling heterogeneous
model settings is not a claim of universal statistical supe-
riority. In the original fixed-64 primary experiment, the
slowest rate resolves only 4/80 panels at 128 episodes and
15/80 at 512. Architectural disclosure does not remove
low signal-to-noise limitations.
5.3.4 A measured tail interval under the
stronger audit
When rates are genuinely public, the unseen-tail mass in
equation (60) is itself a linear contrast ofβ. UsevH =
(0, 0, (1−η1)H,..., (1−ηd)H)in equation (63). Intersect
itsintervalwith[0 ,Q max(1−η0)H]. Anemptyintersection
is a model conflict, not an interval silently clipped into
feasibility. Splittingα= 0.05equally between the gain
and tail gives at least 95% joint coverage per panel by a
union bound.
This analysis reuses the primary observations; it cre-
ates no additional independent market data. ForH = 64,
the joint event holds in 2,796/2,880 panels (97.08%).
There are nine model conflicts. At a fixed observation
length of 64, the statistical upper bounds below are medi-
ans across eighty panels; they are not worst-case bounds
for every episode or every institution.

5.3.5 Assumption violations remain visible
Five eighty-panel controls evaluate deliberately ungated
inference. Omitting a slow state produces eighty false
stable classifications in eighty panels. Omitting a positive
inventory-feedback channel produces three false stable
classifications in eighty panels. Noisy regressors and a
violated reset produce no wrong labels in their particular
runs, but their calibration theorem is still unavailable;
absence of observed errors is not proof of robustness. The
valid control produces eighty correct unstable classifica-
tions. All outcomes, including unhelpful and non-failing
controls, are retained. A fit cannot authenticate the
architecture that made its confidence statement valid.
6 Funded Market Mechanism:
Solvency vs. Signal Stability
6.1 An account-consistent market envi-
ronment
Stability of the price-signal recursion (ρ(J) < 1) is a
statement about prices, not about the balance sheets that
supply liquidity. This section builds a funded simulation
in which the two can be compared.
Liquidity providers face cash, inventory and leverage
limits. In the model below a dealer can reach those
limits while the price-feedback recursion is stable, and
it then stops absorbing flow. To study this gap between
signal stability and dealer capacity, we embed the learning
dynamics in a funded, double-entry dealer market with
explicit cash and inventory ledgers.
6.1.1 Orders, price formation and adaptive de-
mand
Let Pt = p∗+xt, with the constant reference value
p∗= 100dollars. Chartist i holds a private smoothed
displacementmi,t, with demandvi,t =χimi,t. A funda-
mentalist submitsvF,t =−φxt, a noise trader submits
vN,t, and the experiment’s trader submitsut. The dealer
absorbs their net quantityVt. Prices and memories up-
date as
Vt =
∑
i
vi,t +vF,t +vN,t +ut, P t+1 =P t +κVt,
(64)
mi,t+1 = (1−ηi)mi,t +ηixt.(65)
Writinga = 1−κφ, qi = κχi and yi,t = κχimi,t gives
(29) exactly. Updates usext, not the newly movedxt+1;
changing timing changes the response recurrence. Agents
have adaptive memory, not trained neural policies or
demonstrated utility-optimal behavior.
Empirical order-book research motivates considering
a local relation between net pressure, price changes and
depth, but does not validate this specific aggregate-
demand model or its chosen parameters [50]. In par-
ticular, actual order-flow imbalance includes quote and
cancellation events; the simple signed trade volumeVt
here is not claimed to equal that empirical measure.
6.1.2 Double-entry accounting and economic
scope
Trades execute at the within-step midpointPex
t = (Pt +
Pt+1)/2, with half-spreadc = 0.005dollars transferred
to the dealer. Each nondealer actor with quantityv has
changes
∆N=v,∆C=−vP ex
t −c|v|.
The dealer has the opposite aggregate inventory and
cash changes. Thus total shares and cash transfers sum
to zero after every step; marking all inventories to one
common quote also preserves aggregate marked wealth
when net initial shares are zero. The cash variables are
ledger changes, not evidence that all actors satisfy capital
requirements. Unconstrained credit and short positions,
fixed depth, no defaults and no endogenous balance-sheet
limits make the primary model a local mechanism test
rather than a complete financial economy.
The primary price impact is0.02dollars per share and
a = 0.4, implying φ= 30shares per dollar. Chartist
elasticities are set from the declaredqi. Background
net-flow noise has standard deviation 0.10 shares; quote
observation error has standard deviation 0.002 dollars;
each initial private memory has standard deviation 0.01
dollars. The estimator is not told these scales. Time is
an abstract model step, not a claimed number of market
seconds. No live-market orders, customer records or
external financial data are used.
6.1.3 Experimental design and information fire-
wall
The frozen primary panel uses rates(0.5,ηs)with ηs∈
{0.002,0.02,0.10}andG∈{0.70,0.95,1.05,1.30}. Sixty
percent of normalized feedback is fast and forty percent
slow. The imposed class floor is η= ηs; this is pub-
lic design-side information, not an estimated individual
hidden rate. The equality at the floor is a benchmark
boundary choice, and off-grid support is separately tested.
For each of 12 configurations, 128 or 1,024 indepen-
dent episodes use pulses of one or four shares. Forty
independent panels per configuration/design give 1,920
panels. Each episode records 66 quote changes. Inference
at largest lags 8, 24 and 64 gives 5,760 horizon rows.
There areH + 1response coefficients through lagH; the
last collected coefficient, lag 65, is unused in these fits
but included in the simultaneous confidence family, con-
servatively. There are 1,105,920 episodes and 72,990,720
quoted-price observations, not that many independent
financial markets.
26

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 27 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
Only a public object with keysprobes and prices
crosses into estimation. Analytical responses, latent tra-
jectories, true gains and configuration labels are used
by the evaluation layer only. Known noise standard de-
viations and exact direct persistence are not estimator
arguments. Full public response estimates, endpoints
and standard errors are saved for every panel, together
with generation seeds and raw-record hashes. Four pub-
lic episode examples and their response covariances are
included. All other raw episodes can be regenerated; they
are not represented as external market records.
6.2 Binding financial constraints,
balance-sheet stress, and default
risk
6.2.1 A funded transaction model, not just a
ledger
Version 0.4 records trades but does not impose bind-

a background-flow trader, a probe account and a dealer.

fers. This leaves total cash and shares unchanged. The
price then moves byλ∑
ivi; a declared external repricing
may follow.
For previously feasible participants, requested orders
are projected onto
|I′

i, C ′
i≥−κiEi,0.(67)
These constraints include spread costs in post-trade eq-
uity. For a fixed trade sign they are linear inequalities
in the fill, so the nearest feasible fill along the requested
trade direction is computed explicitly. A separate LP
verifier checks 500 random cases against this projection.
A breach caused by subsequent price marking triggers
forced liquidation on the next step. Negative equity is
recorded as default; outstanding debt is not erased or
replaced with fresh capital.
The dealer also faces inventory, leverage and credit lim-
its. When desired aggregate flow exceeds its capacity, all
participant fills are proportionally reduced. If its existing
account is already infeasible, the simulated venue halts
rather than inventing an unconstrained outside buyer.
Existing positions remain on the books. An externally
imposed price shock is still recorded. The maximum
leverage observedaftera price move may exceed the exe-
cution limit; this is precisely why margin calls exist. The
accounting audit distinguishes infeasible newly accepted
trades from later marking losses.
Funding–liquidity feedback is established financial the-
ory [8]. Trading under propagator and inequality con-
straints also has dedicated predecessors [55]. The present
simulator is a simple executable validation environment
for the measurement distinction. It is not a new liquidity-
spiral theory, an exchange order book, or an implementa-
tion of those papers’ optimal-execution algorithms.
6.2.2 Primary economic settings and accounting
audit
The base initial equities, in account order, are
(100, 100, 600, 300, 600, 2000)dollars. Leverage limits are
two; credit limits equal initial equity; inventory caps are
(3, 3, 15, 10, 10, 30)shares. The ample regime multiplies
equity and inventory caps by twenty. The tight regime
uses the base values. The unlimited counterfactual ig-
nores execution constraints and may accumulate insolvent
positions; it is a diagnostic counterfactual, not a viable
funding arrangement. Positions initially sum to zero.
The primary panel crossesG∈{0.7, 1.3}, slow rates
0.02/0.1, the three funding regimes, and repricing shocks
of zero or minus two dollars at step 16. Each of 24 settings
has 20,480 episodes of 64 transitions. The probe buys or
sells two shares at step zero and requests the reverse at
step eight: four gross requested shares, not a guaranteed
full fill. External random tapes are shared across funding
counterfactuals. Therefore 491,520 setting-trajectories
use 163,840 unique exogenous episode tapes; they must
not be counted as 491,520 mutually independent market
shocks.
The execution engine modifies 12,388,550 order re-
quests and records 2,240,614 account-step margin calls.
These are repeated order/account-step counts, not unique
crises. Maximum cash-conservation error is2.41×10−10
dollars, maximum share error2.34×10−12shares, and
maximum fresh-trade constraint residual3.19×10−12.
Thus the financial limits are active, rather than decorative
parameters.
6.2.3 Finite-horizon risk without an unjustified
linear certificate
When accounts constrain fills, the dynamics are state-
dependent and nonlinear. The audited memory bound
still describes that isolated signal module, but not all feed-
back in the financially constrained system. We therefore
do not apply the linear gain interval to certify funded-
system stability.
Instead define an episode eventB as any account de-
fault, a venue halt, or an adaptive trader’s peak equity
drawdown exceeding 20% of initial equity during 64 tran-
sitions. Price dislocation, margin activity, turnover and
unfilled orders are reported separately. The endpoint is

frozen before the primary experiment. For iid repetitions
of a declared scenario distribution,Kn = ∑n
j=1 1Bj is
binomial regardless of the within-episode nonlinear dy-
namics. An exact Clopper–Pearson interval boundspB
[54].
Proposition 6.1(Planned-budget operational-risk cov-
erage).At predeclared episode budgetsn∈{32, 128, 512},
construct two-sided exact binomial intervals at errorα/3
each. Under iid episodes from the same specified sce-
nario law, all three intervals coverpB with probability at
least1 −α. This conclusion does not require linearity, a
memory-rate floor, or Gaussian observations.
Proof. Each interval inverts binomial tail tests and has
noncoverage probability at mostα/3. A union bound
over the three planned looks gives total noncoverage
at mostα, without requiring independence across the
nested budgets. Arbitrary additional peeking, dependent
episodes, a changed scenario distribution, or an event
definition chosen after seeing losses is outside the claim.
The illustrative acceptance thresholdpB = 5%is a
research choice, not a regulatory standard or investment
recommendation. At zero events, the upper endpoint
is1 −(α/6)1/n. Thus 32 quiet episodes are insufficient
to establish risk below 5%; 128 can be sufficient. There
are 960 setting-panels evaluated at three nested budgets,
yielding 2,880 rows. In the primary settings, the realized
event frequencies are extreme: twenty settings have zero
events and four unlimited-funding shocked settings have
an event in every episode. At 128 episodes all panels
consequently receive a definite probability-threshold label.
This isnota difficult near-threshold benchmark, not
proof of universal 128-episode practicality, and not a
demonstration of zero population risk in the zero-event
settings. Exact coverage is separately enumerated on
3,003(n,p)grid points.
6.2.4 Dealerstress: locallystablesignalfeedback
is not system safety
The first financial panel activates participant constraints
but has no dealer halts. A separately labeled post-
primary extension therefore starts the tight-regime dealer
with 19 shares and equity 1,000, balanced by positions
of minus nine shares in the fundamentalist account and
minus ten in the probe account. Cash is adjusted to
preserve each initial equity and zero net shares. These
positions satisfy the initial constraints. Repricing at step
16 has mean minus two or minus ten dollars and indepen-
dent standard deviation one. Eight settings each contain
8,192 fresh episodes, totaling 65,536.
For G = 0.7, the unconstrained linear quantity is
a+ ∑qi = 0.82<1, yet substantial halt frequencies oc-
cur under the stressed dealer balance sheet. This supplies
a concrete model-specific counterexample to transferring
the signal-stability label to operational financial safety.
The difference is not attributed exclusively to artificial in-
telligence: ordinary adaptive demand and standard fund-
ing constraints are enough. Neither the higher-gain nor
lower-gain case is uniformly safer across these outcomes.
No blanket prescription for capital limits or autonomous-
agent adoption follows.
Dealer capacity-risk surface and inventory-
buffer scaling.Table 16 varies inventory capac-

High-power verification (N = 1,000per cell).To
verify that the sampling uncertainties of the N =
30pilot do not distort asymptotic inference, we exe-
cute a high-power Monte Carlo validation across all
22 parameter cells with N = 1,000independent dis-
turbance trajectories per cell (22,000total simulated
paths; simulations/high_power_funded_dealer.py).
The high-power evaluation shrinks the95%Clopper–
Pearson binomial confidence interval from±18.5%down
to±1.5%. Under high-power sampling, the intermediate
transition cell at S = 0.70,0 .5×capacity, µ=−0.2
exhibits a69.1%failure rate ([66.1%, 72.0%]), while de-
terministic boundary cells (0.0%and100 .0%) narrow
to[0 .0%, 0.4%]and[99 .6%, 100.0%]respectively. This
confirms that dealer inventory exhaustion under large fun-
damental shocks is a structural feature of balance-sheet-
constrained liquidity provision rather than finite-sample
noise.
