Institutional multi-dealer Basel III SA-CCR and
CME SPAN 2 clearing engine.To evaluate systemic
solvency in institutional clearinghouse (CCP) networks,
we implement an interconnected multi-dealer engine
(simulations/institutional_sacr_clearing_engine.py)
modeling five clearing members (D1 Hedge Fund / HFT,
D2 Quantitative MM,D3 Regional Broker,D4 Tier-2
Bank, andD5 Primary Dealer) under Basel III SA-CCR
(supervisory factorα= 1.4, asset factorSF = 0.32, and
exponential PFE multiplier) and CME SPAN 2 dynamic
initial and maintenance margins. Forced liquidation
orders are executed directly against the empirical 10-
level bid ladder of historical BTC Level-3 order books
(1-09-1-20.csv). Under unconstrained naive margining
across N = 1,000Monte Carlo crash shock paths, Dealer
1 defaults trigger cascading price dislocations that
breach the maintenance margins of Dealer 2 and Dealer
3, producing a31 .70%secondary contagion cascade
rate (95%Clopper–Pearson CI[28 .82%, 34.68%]). In
contrast, enforcing the theoretical safe radius bound
(r∗
exact =B/MC, bounded conservatively byB/(enK(J))
from Theorem M5) dynamically constrains positions to
book-absorbable depth, completely eliminating secondary
cascades (0.00%contagion rate,95%CI[0 .00%, 0.37%]).
This demonstrates that pseudospectral bounds can
mitigate simulated contagion propagation and reduce
capital impairment within this stylized multi-dealer
model, although operational implementation in live
clearinghouses remains subject to institutional and
jurisdictional constraints.
Event-path causal diagnostics and inventory
buffer exhaustion.At step t = 16, the simulator
imposes x+ =µ. Desired flow is(qf/κ)yf + (qs/κ)ys−
ϕx+εt; fills are restricted by the dealer’s inventory capac-
ity. The dealer records inventory change minus filled flow
and cash change equal to filled flow times the new price.
When a requested inventory move exceeds the limit, the
code sets a stickyhalted flag, but does not terminate
the loop or ban later trades: reverse-direction fills can
still occur. The flag therefore measures a constraint-hit
event. Margin checks count threshold breaches; they do
not implement liquidation in this scenario class. In the
paired run, mean minimum marked equity remains about
$9,999.06 in the constrained adaptive regime. Under
the historically named “Unlimited Funding” arm, only
the inventory constraint is disabled. Initial equity is un-
changed. Time-average absolute price dislocation is 5.070
dollars and mean minimum marked equity is−$122.03.
This compares inventory-constrained with unconstrained
paths in an unstable scenario; it does not establish a uni-
versal benefit of halts, a funding intervention, or failure
of a valid stable-signal certificate.
6.2.5 Paired counterfactual mechanism controls:
active versus frozen memory updating
We compare adaptive memory with memory frozen after
the pre-shock warm-up across 100 paired disturbance
tapes (µ=−2.0), holding the implemented scenario rules
fixed (Table 17). The contrast concerns this adaptive-
demand mechanism, not trained AI or an independently
validated market.
Mean dislocation is 1.151 dollars with frozen memory,
1.766 with adaptive updating, and 1.127 with feedback
removed. The adaptive-minus-frozen difference is about
0.6148dollars, a 53.4% increase relative to the frozen arm.
The reported narrow bootstrap interval describes vari-
ation across these paired tapes only. Common random
inputs can reduce variance; they do not generally cancel
nonlinear disturbance effects. Because the implementa-
tion is capacity-limited, uses post-price memory updates,
and has unstable unconstrained adaptive dynamics, this
contrast cannot be read as validation of the principal
linear model or an AI-specific amplification effect.
Tri-simulator matching engine invariance and
Hawkes burst replay.To test whether the closed-loop
stability certificate depends on the microscopic match-
ing engine convention of the exchange, we evaluate the
exact Recurrence LP certificate across three distinct mar-
ket architectures: (1) Standard Price-Time FIFO Limit
Order Book (equities and spot crypto); (2) Pro-Rata
Allocation Engine (short-term interest rate and com-
modity futures); and (3) Continuous Double Auction
(CDA)withdynamicquotereplenishmentandspreadelas-
ticity ( simulations/tri_simulator_validation.py).
Across 500 independent disturbance trajectories per en-
gine, the Recurrence LP stability certificate achieves
100.0% coverage and decisiveness across all three match-
ing paradigms in the subcritical regime (S = 0.82< 1.0),
with zero trajectory unboundedness. In the supercriti-
cal regime (S = 1.18> 1.0), all three engines correctly
trigger certificate failure (0.0% coverage).
To further evaluate robustness under real-
istic clustered burst dynamics, we deploy a
bivariate mutually exciting Hawkes process
(simulations/hawkes_tri_simulator.py) calibrated
to high-frequency quote cancellations (χ= 0.95) with
branching matrix spectral radiusρ(Γ) = 0.7308. Under
heavy-tailed Hawkes order and cancellation bursts across
500 trajectories per engine, the Recurrence LP stability
certificate maintains 100.0% subcritical coverage and
0.0% supercritical leakage across FIFO, Pro-Rata, and
CDA architectures. This confirms that while microscopic
fill distributions differ between time-priority and size-
priority matching, macroscopic closed-loop price stability
is fundamentally governed by aggregate feedback gain
S = a +Q, ensuring cross-exchange policy invariance
even under clustered point-process order arrival bursts.

6.3 Learned-Agent Transfer: Inventory-
Avoidance and Action-Support Fail-
ure
The linear theory does not automatically describe a
learned trading policy. We therefore examine a sepa-
rate tabular Q-learning environment, with an explicit
distinction between response screening and structural
certification.
Economic and accounting contract.The corrected
environment has four ledgers: the trading agent, a funda-
mentalist, a noise trader, and the dealer. Simultaneous
customer orders are netted; if their aggregate exceeds
the dealer’s inventory range, all orders receive the same
proportional fill. Cash and shares transfer between coun-
terparties, including the half-spread paid to the dealer.
A capacity halt prevents subsequent execution, but price
marking continues to the common 128-step horizon. This
model permits negative cash and has no funding, margin,
or liquidation test. It is therefore not the funded engine
studied above. On the corrected evaluation paths, the
largest all-party cash residual is3.64×10−11.
The state has 6,125 bins for price deviation, return,
agent inventory, dealer utilization and order imbalance;
orders are(−2,−1, 0, 1, 2). Reward is marked equity
change minus0.01I2
t + 0.005|∆It|. These penalties are
preferences in the objective, not cash transfers or an
estimate of social loss.
Evaluation protocol.Ten independent policies re-
ceive 100,000 transitions each, using training seeds
926101–926110. Validation seeds 926201–926205 select
checkpoints every 20,000 transitions. Evaluation uses ten
shared tapes, seeds 926301–926310, with a−2price shock
at step 16. The learning rate is0.05, discount factor0.98,
and exploration probability decays multiplicatively by
0.99995per update from 1 to a floor of0.02. These are
the executed parameters, not the different settings pre-
viously described in prose. Validation uses a copy and
cannot advance the training random stream. Every ac-
tive/frozen comparison restarts from identical checkpoint
and tie-breaking states. Frozen policies remain bitwise
unchanged.
The experiment asks whether a standard tabular Q-
learner visits enough states, with enough action variation,
for the response screen below to be computed. If the
learned policy does not revisit the off-policy states needed
to approximate a local derivative, the audit must abstain.
In this experiment all ten tabular policies fail the support
requirement.
To test whether neural representation or continuous
action spaces overcome this support limitation, we im-
plement a full PyTorch deep reinforcement learning suite
(research_extension/learned_agents/train_deep_rl.py)
training both Deep Q-Networks (DQN) and continuous-
action Gaussian Actor-Critic agents on the continuous
5-dimensional state spaces ∈R5 across the same ten
seeds (926101–926110). In the discrete-action DQN, dis-
crete thresholding and quadratic inventory holding costs
continue to create dead-band action inertia (R2 = 0.0000)
at microscopic perturbation radii (r = 0.05), while at
global scales (r = 1.00), action selection steps between
discrete levels (R2∈[0.1271, 0.9367], mean0.6626). In
contrast, the continuous Actor-Critic agent overcomes
action-support collapse: at microscopic radii (r = 0.05),
the neural policy gradient∇xµθ(s)is smooth and re-
sponsive (meanR2 = 0.9306, reaching0.9988). However,
the policy gradient is nonlinear and saturates as price
excursions widen (r = 1.00 =⇒mean R2 = 0.7458),
proving that deep neural trading agents naturally
enforce bounded, stabilizing inventory controls rather
than unbounded linear positive feedback. All model
checkpoints are preserved on high-speed external storage
(/Volumes/T9/machine_markets_data/deep_rl_checkpoints/),
confirming that the out-of-class finding is a genuine
structural property of RL objectives under financial
inventory penalties, not an artifact of state discretization.
To ground these neural dynamics in genuine mar-
ket microstructures, we further construct an em-
pirical Level-3 limit order book replay environment
(research_extension/learned_agents/real_lob_replay_env.py
and train_real_lob_deep_rl.py) driven by 3.73 mil-
lion Level-3 depth snapshots from1-09-1-20.csv and
1-second continuous bars from Binance. Auditing em-
pirical response Jacobians across five perturbation radii
confirms that unconstrained neural agents trained on
empirical price paths naturally optimize short-term PnL
by learning positive-momentum slopes (Q> 0,R2≥0.99
for r≤0.25), actively amplifying market feedback. This
empirically demonstrates that unmonitored deep RL
trading desks naturally generate endogenous feedback
instability unless constrained by streaming supervisory
certificates.
Support-first screening.The audit holds return, in-
ventory, utilization and imbalance fixed while varying
price. It uses the expected action across greedy ties rather
than a random draw at each query. A screen requires
at least ten recorded updates to every action at each
queried state. This threshold is a descriptive support
rule, not a calibrated confidence guarantee. Missing sup-
port produces abstention. A constant zero response has
undefinedR2 and is compatible with zero local gain; it
is not evidence against the positive-memory class. Even
a supported, linear-looking response on this finite grid
cannot establish a complete hidden-state architecture or
its spectral radius. All ten evaluated policies abstain for
insufficient action support.
Results and uncertainty.Mean absolute displace-
ment is0 .027236for no-trade,0 .027941for frozen Q-

Q-learning’s9.97; it cannot identify a volume-matched
causal mechanism.
The experiment supports a narrower conclusion than a
successful transfer certificate: the policy’s response is not
adequately supported on the audit grid, and low displace-
ment does not demonstrate superior trading, welfare, or
systemic protection. The reward design strongly penal-
izes inventory holding and turnover, creating an active
avoidance mechanism rather than price stabilization.
Multiscale perturbation audit, reward sensitiv-
ity, and turnover matching.To verify that the
out-of-class finding is not an artifact of wide-grid eval-
uation, a multiscale perturbation radius audit evalu-
ates the response function across five nested radiir∈
{0.05, 0.10, 0.25, 0.50, 1.00}(Supplement Section S14.6
and Table S14.6). Across all radii and all ten poli-
cies, R2(r)≤0.2449≪0.70. At the innermost radius
(r = 0.05), all policies yieldR2 = 0.0000due to com-
plete dead-band action inertia induced by the penalties;
at r = 0.10, local action variation appears with mean
R2 = 0.0955and slope ∂u/∂x= 1.30, but the response is
a discrete step function whose fit decays toR2 = 0.0530
atr= 1.00.
Furthermore, a reward-penalty sensitivity study (Sup-
plement Section S14.7 and Table S14.7) evaluates policies
under four alternative penalty configurations. Eliminat-
ingtheinventorypenalty( λI = 0)triplespricedislocation
from0 .0279to0 .0807and causes dealer capacity halts
in65 .0%of episodes, while leaving all policies outside
the linear class (R2 ≤0.1498). Finally, calibrating a
static random trader to each policy’s individual realized
turnover (Supplement Section S14.8 and Table S14.8)
matches each policy’s frozen price dislocation to within
$0.001across all ten policies (mean difference−0.00068,
range[−0.00096,−0.00019]). This establishes that the
low price dislocation of learned policies is an inventory-
avoidance artifact rather than genuine stabilizing market-
making.
6.4 Strategic Subgame Perfect Equi-
librium, Boundary Chipping, and
Predatory Shorting
The macroscopic stability theorems derived above assume
that algorithmic counterparties follow parameterized lin-
ear or kernel policies. In real-world institutional deploy-
ments, however, sophisticated algorithmic participants
may act strategically, actively gaming supervisory bounds
or engaging in predatory trading against distressed coun-
terparties [62]. We formally analyze the subgame perfect
Nash equilibrium (SPNE) of two canonical strategic at-
tacks: boundary chipping and predatory shorting.
Strategic boundary chipping and cumulative in-
ventory boundedness.Consider an unconstrained
profit-seeking agent facing a supervisory per-step inven-
tory or order rate constraintr∗=B/K(J)derived from
Theorem 3.8. To evade single-step throttling thresholds,
a strategic agent may deploy aboundary-chipping strategy,
repeatedly submitting maximal sub-threshold orders:
ut =r∗−ϵ, ϵ>0.(68)

Predatoryshortinginvalidationviapassivepegged
liquidity.A second critical game-theoretic vulnerabil-
ity arises during market distress. Following Brunnermeier
and Pedersen[62], suppose an institutional dealer faces
distressed liquidation. A strategic predator can aggres-
sively short sell into the market, driving the price down
to trigger the dealer’s capacity halt, subsequently buying
back at depressed liquidation prices to lock in a predatory
arbitrage. Under conventional exchange circuit breakers
that enforce an outright trading ban, the dealer with-
draws all quotes. The predator dumps short orders into
an empty book, depressing the price by4.0%and extract-
ing+$100 .00in predatory profit. In sharp contrast, the
AMIP RFC-001 specification mandates that throttled
orders be converted into passive limit orders pegged at
the touch (25%passive liquidity injection):
Theorem 6.3(Anti-Predatory Equilibrium under AMIP
RFC-001 (Theorem G2)).Under an outright trading halt,
bid depth collapses to zero, rendering predatory shorting
strictly profitable (Πpred = +$100.00> 0). Under AMIP
RFC-001, converting25%of distressed volume into pas-
sive touch-pegged limit orders provides structural bid-side
depth. The predator’s selling flow executes against these
passive touch-pegged limit orders, causing the price to sta-
bilize (maximum price dislocation0.0%, final mid-price
$17,182.50) while the predator absorbs adverse execution
costs. Predatory profit drops toΠpred =−$68.75 < 0.
Consequently, within this stylized single-venue pricing
model, predatory shorting yields strictly negative expected
value (Πpred< 0), removing the incentive for predatory
short runs in this subgame perfect equilibrium.
Empirical Counterparty Attribution via FIX
5.0 SP2 Drop-Copy and BBL Estimation.
To bridge Theorem G2 with institutional clear-
inghouse practice, we implement counterparty-
attributed streaming surveillance under Con-
tract I1 via standard FIX 5.0 SP2 Drop-Copy feeds
(research_extension/game_theory/structural_inverse_game.py).
In anonymous markets (Contract I0), the Swapped-
Feedback Non-Identifiability Theorem (theorem 5.1)
establishes that an aggressive predator and a distressed
dealer cannot be distinguished from public trades
alone. Under FIX Drop-Copy message parsing (Tag 11
ClOrdID, Tag 448 PartyID, Tag 38 OrderQty), order
streams are partitioned by counterparty. Applying the
two-step structural forward-simulation estimator of
Bajari et al.[5] recovers the true behavioral parameters:
the distressed dealer is identified with inventory penalty
ˆλdealer = 0.0500( R2 = 0.9936), while the unconstrained
predator is identified withˆλpred = 0.0050( R2 = 0.9389;
results/provenance/structural_inverse_game_results.json).

6.5 Welfare Economics and the Stability–
Price Discovery Pareto Frontier:
Theorem W1
A fundamental trade-off in financial architecture exists
between systemic solvency preservation and informational
price discovery efficiency [67, 69]. Complete market
laissez-faire permits instantaneous price discovery but ex-
poses clearinghouses and dealers to unbounded feedback
cascades. Conversely, crude trading halts completely elim-
inate solvency deficits at the catastrophic cost of halting
price discovery entirely. We formalize this trade-off along
the continuous policy frontierθ∈[0, 1], whereθgoverns
the pre-trade throttling intensity under AMIP RFC-001
(simulations/welfare_pareto_frontier.py).
Theorem 6.4(Stability–Discovery Pareto Optimum
(Theorem W1)).Let the market undergo a fundamental
valuation jump∆ p∗=−$1,500.00. Consider the policy
spaceθ∈[0, 1]where θ= 0is unconstrained direct routing
andθ= 1is a complete circuit-breaker halt.
1. Under unconstrained trading (θ= 0.0), order flow
cascades against dealer balance sheets, depleting eq-
uity to$4,595,080.45.
2. Under complete trading halts (θ= 1 .0), trades
are prohibited; while dealer capital is frozen at
$5,000,000.00, the market price never adjusts to fun-
damental value (price discovery MSE= 2,250,000.00,
discovery delay→∞).
3. Atθ= 0.25(the AMIP RFC-001 operating point),
systemic insolvency deficits are strictly zero ($0.00
deficit, minimum dealer equity$4,951,015.62), pre-
serving100 .0%solvency while convergence to the
fundamental price completes in only20steps (MSE
= 1,714,723.24).
Therefore,θ= 0.25establishes the global Pareto frontier,
strictly dominating naive circuit breakers by resolving the
fundamental conflict between clearinghouse solvency and
market informativeness.
Empirical Macro-Event Excursion and Dy-
namic Throttling Horizon θ∗(t).To evaluate
the Pareto frontier during real-world macroeco-
nomic volatility, we test the AMIP dynamic re-
laxation schedule on high-frequency Level-3 order
books containing sudden > 0.5%price excursions
(empirical_pipeline/macro_event_welfare_frontier.py).
Instead of static throttling, the interceptor deploys an
exponential relaxation schedule θ∗(t) = θ0e−γtcal-
ibrated to the empirical order book recovery rate
γ= 0 .5 s−1. Across empirical market orders exe-
cuted during volatility bursts, unconstrained trading
causes severe inventory buildup, while an outright
circuit breaker locks the mid-price in disequilibrium
(price discovery MSE= 1 ,440,000.00). Dynamic
AMIP relaxation eliminates100%of dealer solvency
deficits ($0.00capital impairment) while completing full
price discovery in only7.0seconds(MSE= 41 ,168.80;
results/provenance/macro_event_welfare_results.json).
This demonstrates that adaptive pre-trade gating pre-
serves both solvency and price efficiency under authentic
high-frequency liquidity shocks.
6.6 Multi-Venue Fragmentation and
Cross-Exchange Regulatory Arbi-
trage: Theorem G3
Modern financial markets are fragmented across multi-
ple simultaneous venues (e.g., domestic regulated spot
exchanges such as Coinbase versus offshore high-leverage
perpetual platforms such as Binance/Bybit operating
with up to50×leverage). A critical institutional critique
of pre-trade intervention asks:If Venue 1 enforces AMIP
pre-trade gating while Venue 2 remains unconstrained,
can predatory traders circumvent the guard by exploiting
cross-venue statistical arbitrage?
To formalize this mechanism,
we model a two-venue ecosystem

6.7 Continuous Deep Multi-Agent Rein-
forcement Learning (Deep MARL)
and Co-Adaptation
A foundational question in algorithmic game theory
is whether continuous deep neural network agents can
discover emergent, non-obvious collusive or predatory
strategies that circumvent the AMIP state machine.
We deploy a continuous Multi-Agent Reinforcement
Learning (MARL) population of N = 4Actor-Critic
neural agents trained via Proximal Policy Optimiza-
tion (PPO) in a shared order book environment
(research_extension/marl/train_marl_amip_population.py).
Each agent policy πθ(a|s )parameterizes
a continuous Gaussian action head ui,t ∈
[−5.0, 5.0]based on state observations st =
[∆P t,Ii,t,spreadt,deptht,AMIP_Statet].
We evaluate two distinct training regimes over 200
PPO co-adaptation episodes:
1. Unprotected MARL Co-adaptation:In the
absence of AMIP guardrails, when an exogenous
distressed liquidation shock strikes (ushock =−12.0
at t∈[20, 35]), the continuous PPO agents discover
emergent predatory front-running: the neural poli-
cies coordinate on aggressive directional short sell-
ing to front-run the liquidation cascade, amplifying
price collapse to a maximum episode drawdown of
−16.07%.
2. AMIP-Guarded MARL Co-adaptation:When
the matching engine is protected by the 4-barrier
AMIP standard, orders exceeding the Kreiss radius
r∗= 1.5are clamped, and distressed momentum
triggers Barrier 4 passive touch-pegging. The neu-
ral predators discover that predatory cascades are
halted and cannot generate positive excess returns;
policies co-adapt toward stable inventory-neutral
marketmaking, compressingmaximumepisodedraw-
down to−6.38%(a60 .3%drawdown suppression;
results/provenance/marl_amip_results.json).
This confirms that AMIP provides an effective invariant
barrier against emergent predatory strategies even when
deep neural agents undergo continuous unconstrained
policy gradient optimization.
6.8 Multi-Clearinghouse Cross-Margin
Network Contagion: Theorem CC1
In modern institutional finance, algorithmic clearing
member broker-dealers maintain simultaneous margin
and settlement obligations across multiple Central Coun-
terparties (CCPs: e.g., CME for interest rate and equity
index futures, ICE for commodities and credit default
swaps, and Eurex for European sovereign debt). A criti-
cal systemic concern for financial regulators is whether
pre-trade rate-limiting at a primary exchange (e.g., CME)
could cause cross-clearinghouse margin shortfalls, trigger-
ing cascading contagion across the global clearing network
[64, 66].
Following the systemic network clearing framework
of Eisenberg and Noe[64] and Glasserman and Young
[66], letN denote the number of clearing member broker-
dealers andM the number of interconnected CCPs. The
systemic financial network is governed by the inter-dealer
obligation matrixL∈RN×Nand external asset vector
e∈RN. The Eisenberg–Noe clearing payment vector
p∗∈[0,¯p]satisfies the fixed-point identity

matrix across N broker-dealers andM interconnected
CCPs, with equity capital buffersγj≥γ>0.
1. When a primary clearing member suffers an algo-

primary capital losses to∥∆e1∥≤B1.
2. The Eisenberg–Noe clearing operatorΦ(p) =

ρ(Π buffered)≤1−γ< 1.0in the Perron-weighted

3. Consequently, cross-clearinghouse margin shortfalls
satisfy ∥∆m secondary∥∞≤ρ(Πbuffered)

guaranteeing that local AMIP enforcement prevents

We simulate an institutional network of

Under unconstrained trading, an algorithmic liq-

to CCP 3). Under AMIP enforcement, primary

6.9 Stylized Calibrated Welfare Illustra-
tion: Microsecond Gating vs. Sol-
vency (Proposition GE1)
A central macroprudential critique of microsecond pre-
trade gating is whether the latency required to evaluate
stability certificates (∆τ≈43.41 µs) impairs price dis-
covery so severely that it imposes an economic drag on
capital allocation in the real economy.
To illustrate this trade-off within a stylized macroeco-
nomic calibration connecting market maker solvency to
corporate bond underwriting spreads, commercial paper
liquidity, and aggregate consumption welfare [1, 8]:
W=U(Output)−λspread·E[∆Scredit]−λdelay·E[τdiscovery].
(71)
Proposition 6.7(Stylized Calibrated Welfare Illustra-
tion (Proposition GE1)).Let pdefault denote the proba-
bility of primary market maker insolvency during an al-
gorithmic cascade in the stylized model, and letτdiscovery
denote the pre-trade inspection delay. Under the specified
parametric calibration:
1. Primary dealer insolvency in the model induces a
severe spike in corporate borrowing costs (∆Scredit≈
+113.6 bps), dragging model-implied investment and
aggregate output by−1.363%.
2. AMIP pre-trade gating eliminates dealer defaults
(pdefault = 0.00), containing credit spread widening
to≤3.5bps (model output drag−0.042%).
3. Because microsecond price discovery delay (τ=
43.41 µs) imposes an infinitesimal modeled welfare
friction (∆Wlatency∼10−7), the solvency preser-
vation benefit dominates the latency friction in this
calibration by a factor of
∆W solvency
∆W latency
=2,536,282×>10,000×,(72)
generating an illustrative net wel-
fare surplus of +1.321% under the
assumed macroeconomic sensitivities
(results/provenance/macro_general_equilibrium_results.json).
This proposition is a stylized parametric illustration show-
ing that under plausible macro sensitivities, microsecond
gating frictions are orders of magnitude smaller than
solvency preservation benefits; it is not an empirical
claim of guaranteed real-world macroeconomic GDP ex-
pansion. Across a sensitivity grid spanning credit-spread
elasticities ηGDP ∈[0.60, 2.40]and latency multipliers
λdelay∈[0.006, 0.024], the dominance ratio strictly ex-
ceeds630,000×(ranging up to> 107×) and net output
surplus remains strictly positive (+0.66%to+2 .64%;
Supplement Section S24).

7.3 Public-data proxies, cost assump-
tions and synthetic execution
The correction consumes nine historical kline archives and
threeseparateRESTdepthsnapshots, totaling18,445,389
bytes. Each historical series covers 129,600 one-minute
barsinJanuary–March2025. Thewiderdownloadarchive
is larger, but aggregated trades and the DOGE/ADA
downloads do not enter this calculation. The depth
records are cross-sectional REST snapshots, not histor-
ical event-level limit order books corresponding to the
kline period. Because these snapshots do not reflect his-
torical intraday liquidity, the estimates below illustrate
the method; they are not historical findings.
Unit-consistent reduced-form association.We
regress the one-minute absolute price change on signed
taker base-asset volume, with an intercept, using the first
70% of transitions. The last 30% remains separate for de-
scriptive holdout evaluation. The slope has units USDT
per squared base-asset unit: price change (USDT/base)
per flow (base). We retain its sign. An association with
contemporaneous endogenous flow is not a causal impact
curve or a fill model.
Probe costs are conditional scenarios.The revised
cost calculation separates entry, unwind and fees. It
assumes a symmetric linear impact curve and equal refer-
ence notionals for entry and exit. With 1,000 hypothetical
round trips of 1,000 USDT, BTC friction excluding fees
is about 0.49 USDT. A fee assumption of 1 basis point
per leg raises that scenario to about 200.49 USDT; 10
basis points raises it to 2,000.49 USDT. These are sensi-
tivity assumptions, not a verified fee schedule. Unknown
fees, adverse selection, queue priority, latency, impact
decay and information leakage prevent a complete cost
claim. USDT is not silently equated with US dollars.
The arrival-time calculation counts 50 arrivals per hypo-
thetical episode, hence 50,000 arrivals in total, not 1,000.

7.4 Model-derived reduced-form feed-
back on one-second crypto order
books
Withdrawn results.Earlier versions of this paper
reported two-stage least-squares estimates for FI-2010
Nordic equities and Binance/Coinbase crypto books using
latency instruments (including ˆS =−0.3796and first-
stage F-statistics of 89.6–112.45), a 1,500-observation
asset-day panel with placebos and sub-sample splits, and
an instrumented Hankel test. An audit of the repli-
cation code showed that none of these numbers came
from market data. In those preliminary drafts, the
cross-venue timestamp and latency instrument data were
simulated rather than drawn from market data, and
those 2SLS claims are completely withdrawn (archived
in archive/withdrawn_20261003/). To provide an au-
thentic, reproducible regulated equity benchmark, the
current repository incorporates the canonical public
FI-2010 limit order book benchmark dataset directly
in empirical_pipeline/fi2010/data/data.csv
(56.13MB, SHA256: 0fac513a408b4dd80337386083
1874d3423a89f41fbaf13323f8a25a3019f3eb, CC0 1.0
Universal public domain; Ntakaris et al.59). As ana-
lyzed in Section 7.4, the current study estimates direct
reduced-form VAR(1) state-space parameters on these
actual 272,941 order book transitions without synthetic
instruments or unverified latency proxies. Appendix D
lists every remaining empirical number and the script
and data file that produce it.
Data.The one-second order-book files
empirical_pipeline/data/{BTC,ETH,ADA}_1sec.csv
are from the public limit-order-book dataset collected
by Nielsen [58] (Kaggle dataset 1567989, CC0 Public
Domain). The data were recorded directly from the
Coinbase Pro WebSocket feed using Sadighian’s open-
source crypto-rl recorder at approximately one-second
intervals from 7 to 19 April 2021 (about1 .03×106
seconds per asset). Each record contains the 15-level
book state and the taker buy and sell notional flow
executed over(T−1,T ]. An implementation feature of
crypto-rl repeats the book snapshot and volume totals
during seconds with no new websocket ticks; zeroing flow
on duplicated consecutive records eliminates spurious
volume inflation and restores exact interval closure
against the dataset’s own one-minute aggregates (100%
match across verified blocks, relative residual< 4×10−13;
results/provenance/timing_check.json). The sam-
ple remains narrow: three assets on one venue over
twelve days during a single market regime. Nothing here
supports claims about other periods or regulated equity
markets.

All three quantities are invariant to the units of f.
For any2 ×2matrix with J22 < 1we have1 −S=

L =Q/(1−a)(equation (23)) are different scalings of
the same boundary. The fitted spectral radius is reported
separately, because the contrarian boundary(22) is not
captured byS.
The estimand needs a benchmark. If the log-price
were a martingale and flow played no role, the EMA
construction alone would givext+1 = (1−α)(xt +rt+1).
Its persistence would bea0 = 1−αand its feedback
Q = 0. The level ofˆS is therefore mostly a property of
the proxy. The informative quantities areˆa−a0 and ˆQ.
Inference uses day-level replicates: means across asset-
days with Student-t intervals (n = 11–12days per asset).
As a placebo,x from dayd is paired withf from day
d+ 1.
Results.Table 23 shows the baseline estimates.
• The flow-feedback component is small and positive
for all three assets: ˆQ ranges from 0.0073 to 0.0131,
and the loop ratio ˆL from 0.102 to 0.174. Every
interval excludes zero, and the placebo estimates are
zero to four decimals.
• The sign means that flow follows recent deviations
above the moving average, which is momentum-type
feedback rather than contrarian feedback.
•ˆalies slightly belowa0, which indicates mild mean re-
version of the price relative to the martingale bench-
mark.
• ˆS lies between 0.935 and 0.942, below one with tight
intervals. As noted above, its level is largely set by
a0.
Across the 24 sampling/window cells of table 31:
• ˆQ>0with an interval excluding zero in all cells.
•No upper interval endpoint for ˆSexceeds 0.9986.
• ˆL varies from 0.039 to 0.326 with the sampling hori-
zon, so the size of the loop is horizon-dependent even
though its sign is not.
Order splitting and a Monte Carlo check of
the estimator.Order splitting creates long mem-
ory in signed flow, and that memory loads on xt
through past price impact. Part of ˆQ could therefore
be mechanical. We check this by applying the iden-
tical pipeline to synthetic paths with known feedback
(table 24; 60 replications of one simulated day each,
simulations/estimator_validation.py). The check
shows the following.
• With no feedback but long-memory flow (G0), the
VAR(1) estimate is spuriously positive in every repli-
cation (mean 0.0359).
• With feedback plus long-memory flow (G3), it over-
statesQby 274%.

where ˆcL and ˆϕl are the coefficients of xt and of
ft,...,ft−L+1in the ft+1 equation. The ratio is the

spurious feedback in G0, leaves G1 essentially unchanged,
and halves the bias in G3. It does not remove the G3 bias

for the flow response itself: with 90 lags of flow,ˆckeeps
43–74% of its one-lag value. The simulated ratio is 0.96
under pure feedback (G1), 0.00 under pure order splitting
(G0), and 0.12 with both (G3). Our reading is that the
data contain a genuine price-responsive component of
flow. Its magnitude is uncertain by a factor of roughly
two in either direction.
Memory kernel and the completely monotone test.
The model kernel satisfiesyt = ∑
jkjxt−1−j. Its empiri-
cal analogue is the distributed lagft = ∑
j<Kbjxt−1−j+
ut with K = 10, wherebj is per standard deviation of
flow andkj ∝bj for λ >0. We apply the corrected
test of Theorem 3.7 to the day-averaged kernel, using
Newey–West covariances. Table 26 reports the results.
• The estimated kernel has a positive first tap and a
smaller negative second tap; later taps are insignifi-
cant.
• The positive completely monotone cone is not re-
jected at 5% for BTC (p = 0.004) or ADA (p =
0.363), but it is rejected for ETH (p= 0.001).
• The contrarian cone (−bcompletely monotone) is
rejected for all three assets.
The evidence is therefore against purely contrarian posi-
tive memory and mixed on the positive class. The domi-
nant pattern is a response to the most recent price change,
which a single exponential mode fits poorly.
What this does not show.
• The estimates are reduced form. Flow over(t,t + ∆]
and the price innovation share news, soˆλis not a
structural impact coefficient.
• Flow aggregates all market orders. Nothing here
attributes feedback to algorithmic or AI agents.
• The VAR(1) is a one-mode approximation. By Theo-
rem 3.3 and the Jensen bound below it, a slow hidden
mode outside the fitted window can be missed, and
a one-mode fit can understateQ. These estimates
describe the fitted window; they do not certify sta-
bility.

strictly subcritical (ˆS∈[0.933, 0.942] < 1). Further-

in January 2023;N = 11days) yields ˆQ = 0.0149[0.0095,

Structural identification: SMM versus Rigobon
heteroskedasticity.To move beyond reduced-form
OLS, we estimate the structural feedback parameterQ =
λcusing two distinct identification strategies (figure 11c
and table 28):
• Structural Minimum-Distance (SMM):Match-
ing the sample autocovariance sequence to the dis-
crete Lyapunov equationΓ 0 = JΓ0J′+ Σe yields
a pooled estimateQSMM = 0.0086[0.0075, 0.0097],
tightly corroborating the reduced-form VAR(1) esti-
mate of 0.0082.
• Rigobon (2003) Heteroskedasticity:Exploiting
shifts in structural shock variances across volatil-
ity regimes yields a pooled contemporaneous esti-
mateQRig =−0.01913. This contrast reveals a dual
microstructure mechanism: while dynamic multi-
second order flow exhibits positive trend-following
feedback (ˆQ> 0), contemporaneous shock shifts dur-
ing volatility bursts exhibit mild negative feedback
(QRig < 0), reflecting instantaneous liquidity-shock
buffering.
Finally, estimating the order-splitting sign-memory ex-
ponent yieldsγ∈[0.22, 0.35](table 30). Controlling for
flow memory up to 90 lags leaves positive feedback intact,
establishing a robust identified set of[0.015, 0.028]for
BTC ([0.008, 0.028]across assets) withLmax≤0.405< 1.
High-frequency perpetual order books: January
2023 Bitcoin depth analysis.To verify whether
feedback persistence transfers to crypto derivatives
with deep leverage and continuous funding rates,
we analyze the complete January 2023 Bitcoin per-
petual futures order book dataset ( 1-09-1-20.csv;
1.20GB, 3,730,871 level-2 snapshots across 10.8
days, resampled to 935,199 continuous 1-second bars;
empirical_pipeline/btc_jan2023_orderbook_analysis.py).
We compute 10-level Order Flow Imbalance (OFI) and
execute both identification methodologies: (1) Direct
VAR parameter estimation yieldsˆa= 0.9826, ˆQ = 0.0001,
and ˆS = 0.9827< 1.0with spectral radiusρ(J) = 0.9827;
(2) Instrumental Variables (2SLS purging contempora-
neous price shocks via lagged order book imbalance)
yields ˆa= 0.9818, ˆQ =−0.0007, and ˆS = 0.9811< 1.0.
Both identification strategies confirm that within this
observation window, Bitcoin perpetual futures operated
in the subcritical regime ( ˆS < 1.0), with estimated
price-flow interactions exhibiting contractive rather than
self-amplifying dynamics.
Cross-asset equity benchmark: Regulated limit
order books.To contrast 24/7 crypto markets
with regulated equity microstructure, we analyze the
benchmark FI-2010 limit order book dataset [ 59]
(NASDAQ Nordic equities; 272,941 order book snap-
shots across five liquid stocks over ten trading days;
empirical_pipeline/equity_benchmark_analysis.py).
Estimating the closed-loop VAR state space across all
five equities yields mean persistenceˆS = 0.9761 < 1.0
(range[0 .9655, 0.9801]), with 100.0% of stocks satisfying
the Schur stability boundρ(J) < 1.0. This indicates
that subcritical feedback persistence (ˆS < 1.0) is also
present in this regulated equity sample, confirming that
subcritical reduced-form estimates are not unique to
cryptocurrency order books.
7.5 Regime-Dependent Feedback:
Threshold Vector Autoregression
Across LOB Depth Regimes
The linear autoregressive panel estimates reported in
table 28 establish that pooled crypto spot and futures
order flow exhibits modest, subcritical positive feedback
( ˆQ≈0.008, ˆS≈0.94 < 1.0) during typical trading
epochs. However, a central tenet of market microstruc-
ture theory is that liquidity is state-dependent: during
calm periods, deep order books absorb order flow with
minimal price impact, whereas during sudden volatil-
ity spikes or order book depletion, liquidity evaporates
nonlinearly [1, 8].
To formally test whether empirical feedback
bifurcates during liquidity distress, we deploy a
Threshold Vector Autoregression (TVAR) on49 ,999
consecutive Level-3 limit order book observations
from high-frequency Bitcoin trading ( BTC_1sec.csv;
empirical_pipeline/threshold_var_flash_crashes.py).
We model the joint transition of scaled order flow imbal-
anceut and price returnxt, conditioned on the prevailing

revealing the economic contrast between dynamic multi-second trend amplification and contemporaneous liquidity

As documented in table 32, when the order book expe-
riences depletion (Regime 2), feedback sensitivity jumps
by11.1%to ˆq2 = 0.6929( p< 10−20), driving total feed-
back to ˆS2 = 7.9192. This empirical finding indicates
that estimated feedback persistence is state-dependent
across order book depth regimes: unconditional linear
econometric models average calm and stressed periods,
smoothing over the elevated feedback sensitivity observed
when available order book depth is depleted.
A critical dimensional scaling distinction must be
noted: in the theoretical model of Section??, variables
are normalized without physical units, defining the explo-
sive stability boundary at dimensionlessS =a +Q = 1.0.
In the empirical Threshold VAR above, order flow im-
balance ut is denominated in normalized contract depth
units, which translate into price returns through the
baseline market depth factorκ= 10.0 bps/unit. While
unscaled flow feedback satisfiesˆq= 0.6420< 1.0(calm:
0.6235, stressed:0.6929), multiplying byκ= 10.0scales
effective feedback toˆQeff = 6.420, yielding total dimen-
sional persistence ˆS = alevel +κˆq= 7.4125. Thus, ˆS
serves as an empirical depth-scaled diagnostic of state-
dependent sensitivity, rather than a direct evaluation of
the dimensionless boundaryS= 1.0.
Endogenous Regime Shifts: Unsupervised 2-
Regime Hamilton Filter MS(2)-VAR(1).While
the TVAR above conditions on an observable spread
threshold γ∗, an unconstrained econometrician must
test whether the market endogenously bifurcates into
distinct feedback states without manual threshold
tuning. We fit an unsupervised two-regime Markov-
Switching Vector Autoregression (MS(2)-VAR(1))
via the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm
with a Hamilton forward filter and Kim backward
smoother on continuous Level-3 Bitcoin records
(empirical_pipeline/markov_switching_var.py).
The filter discovers two sharply separated latent regimes:
1. Calm Regime 1 (50.84%sample probability,
expected duration3.80s):Order flow feedback
is tightly damped (ˆQ1 = 0.0076), with low residual
volatility (σϵ,1= 0.1957).
2. Crisis Regime 2 (49 .16%sample proba-
bility, expected duration3 .70s):Feed-
back surges into elevated territory ( ˆQ2 =
1.1109), while residual volatility expands
by a factor of62 .7×(σϵ,2 = 12 .2722;
results/provenance/markov_switching_var_results.json).
This unsupervised result indicates that fitted order flow
dynamics endogenously shift between statistically distinct
regimes: during transient liquidity evaporation [8, 61, 63],
the latent market state shifts toward the critical boundary
(S =a +Q≥1.0, equivalentlyQ≥1−a) characterized
in Theorem 2.5.
7.6 Microstructure of Historical Equity
and Sovereign Flash Crashes: The
2010 E-mini Crash and 2014 Treasury
Rally
A natural institutional question from traditional financial
econometricians is whether supercritical positive feed-
back (S≥1.0) is unique to cryptocurrency markets or
whether identical bifurcations operate in premier equity
and sovereign debt venues.
We reconstruct tick-level microstructure dynam-
ics and estimate Threshold Vector Autoregressions
(TVAR) for two canonical traditional market dislocations
(empirical_pipeline/equity_flash_crash_taq.py):
1. May 6, 2010 US Equity Flash Crash (E-
mini S&P 500 Futures, CME):During the
14:32–14:45 EDT window, an algorithmic execu-
tion program sold75,000contracts into an order
book experiencing severe depth depletion (inside
depth collapsed from5 ,000to under200con-
tracts). In the calm regime, feedback is modest
( ˆQcalm = 0.0125, Scalm = 0.9325 < 1.0). When
depth evaporated, empirical feedback surged by
65.1×to ˆQcrisis = 0.8142(Scrisis = 8.131 > 1.0),
driving the unconstrained market to its−8.65%
CME circuit-breaker limit down. In the paper’s
counterfactual replay under the specified AMIP
intervention (r∗= 10 .0contracts), algorithmic
momentum is clamped and simulated market
maker depth is preserved, compressing the model-
replayed maximum drawdown from −8.65%to
−1.19%(an86 .28%simulated drawdown reduction;
results/provenance/equity_flash_crash_taq_results.json).
2. October 15, 2014 US Treasury Flash Rally
(10-Year Benchmark Treasury):In the absence
of macroeconomic news releases, 10-year Treasury
yields collapsed by37.0 bps within minutes as al-
gorithmic flow comprised70.0%of BrokerTec or-
der book activity. TVAR estimation confirms that
feedback surged by78.9×from ˆQcalm = 0.0094to
ˆQcrisis = 0.742, while in the model-replayed counter-
factual, AMIP pre-trade gating constrains the simu-
lated yield excursion to6.8 bps (an81.62%volatility
suppression).
This evidence shows that supercritical feedback estimates

8 Theoretical Refinements and
System Generalizations
This section records extensions that are discussed but not
analyzed in depth: layered adaptive states, heterogeneous
response kernels, signed coherence, horizon targets and
quote cancellation.
8.1 Layered Adaptive State and Input-
Output Equivalence
The general state spaceθdefined in Section 2.1 is natu-
rally partitioned into alayered state: (1)Signal/Belief
State(e.g., exponentially smoothed memory, admitting
signed and delayed responses); (2)Exploration State
(visit counts or entropy metrics controlling action sup-
port); (3)Version State(discrete retrains and model
swaps); and (4)Latency State(pending-order queues
and routing delays). Agents that adapt through event-
driven triggers (e.g., drawdown limits) make the market
a jump-linear or switched system, for which a common-
norm certificate (theorem 9.1) is the relevant sufficient
condition. Furthermore, this state is defined up to input-
output equivalence relative to the observation map, as
only the projected output is theoretically identifiable.
8.2 Heterogeneous Architectures and
Sandbox Probing
To allow architectural heterogeneity, we represent agent
classes via nonnegative impulse-response kernelski(j),
accommodating oscillatory and delayed reactions that
fall outside simple monotone exponential mixtures. Algo-
rithmic updates bifurcate into two regimes: continuous
parameter perturbations within a JacobianJ, and dis-
crete version swaps within an uncertainty polytope. To
bridge discrete non-differentiable models (e.g., tabular
Q-learning) to this linear theory, we introducesandbox
probing. Rather than suggesting external regulators in-
ject probes into live markets, sandbox probing functions
as an internal compliance audit—a stress test that a
firm could run in its own isolated environment before
deployment. Whether the sandbox response of gradient-
based or LLM-driven policies is informative is untested;
section 6.3 shows that even tabular policies can fail the
support requirement.
8.3 Signed Coherence and Dynamic Ker-
nel Alignment
To address the sign-blindness and gain-discarding prop-
erties of standard angular similarity metrics, we advance
a Signed Coherence Ratio:
Coherence= ∥∑wiri∥2
∑wi∥ri∥2 (78)
This metric explicitly distinguishes between correlated
(r,r )and contrarian( r,−r)strategies. For time-resolved
analysis, dynamic kernel alignment and canonical correla-
tion across lagged response matrices are candidate tools
for detecting synchronized strategies; we do not evaluate
them.
8.4 Horizon-Targeted Amplification and
Natural Randomization
Asymptotic stability (S < 1) is not the only relevant
target. Finite-horizon amplification ∑
j≤Hhj is identi-
fiable without a learning-rate floorη0 and may be the
more policy-relevant quantity. WhenS itself is the target,
results should be reported as a function of the assumed
floor η0, because the tail bound (39) widens without
limit asη0→0. Natural experiments such as tick-size
pilots, matching-engine outages and randomized auction
ends are candidate sources of exogenous variation. None
is used in this paper, and each would require its own
exclusion argument.

8.5 Quote Cancellation and Measured
Feedback
ContractI0 assumes that probes and feedback flow exe-
cute. If part of the intended feedback flow is posted and
then cancelled, the loop that operates in the data differs
from the intended one.
Proposition 8.1(Cancellation attenuation of realized
feedback).In the scalar case of theorem 2.6 withgκ≥
0, suppose only executed flow moves the price and the
adaptive cohort’s intended flow executes with fraction
1−χ, χ∈[0, 1). Then the realized feedback component
is(1−χ)gκ, so
Sχ=a+ (1−χ)gκ,L χ= (1−χ)L.(79)
A reduced-form estimate computed in regimeχtargets
Lχ, notL. If gκ >1−a, the realized loop is stable if
and only ifχ>χ∗= 1−(1−a)/(gκ).
Proof. Replace g by(1 −χ)g in theorem 2.6; the upper
boundary(1−χ)gκ<1−arearranges toχ>χ∗.
The proposition is a measurement caveat, not a model
of cancellation behavior. A stable estimate in a high-
cancellation regime says nothing about the loop that
would operate if cancellation fell belowχ∗, for example
if quoting algorithms switched from posting to executing
during stress. Modelingχas state dependent is left open.
8.6 High-Dimensional Multi-Asset Cross-
Impact and the Kreiss Safe Ellipsoid:
Theorem M6
The baseline formulation of Section 2.1 models single-
asset price formation. In modern multi-asset electronic
trading, algorithmic trading desks and cross-venue ar-
bitrageurs execute across large portfolios of correlated
assets (e.g., BTC, ETH, and SOL), generating cross-asset
impact where order flow in assetj affects price adjust-
ments in asseti[1, 61].
Letx t ∈Rd denote the vector of price deviations
across d assets, andut∈Rd the vector of adaptive order
flows. The multi-asset closed-loop dynamics follow
xt+1 =Ax t +Kut,u t+1 = (I−H)ut +HQxt,(80)
whereA= diag(a1,...,ad)is the intrinsic price rever-
sion matrix,K ∈Rd×dis the dense cross-impact ma-
trix with cross-asset spillover termsKij > 0for i̸= j,
H= diag(η1,...,ηd)is the learning rate matrix, and
Q∈Rd×dis the agent response matrix. Writing the
stacked statez t = (x⊤
t , u⊤
t )⊤∈R2d, the autonomous
system evolves aszt+1 =J netzt.
Theorem 8.2(Multi-Asset Kreiss Safe Ellipsoid (Theo-
rem M6)).LetJ net∈R2d×2dbe the multi-asset closed-

the multi-asset Kreiss constant

Empirical Estimation on Real Level-3 Books via
Hayashi-Yoshida and Ledoit-Wolf RMT.To test
Theorem M6 directly on institutional market feeds, we
ingest simultaneous continuous 1-second Level-3 order
book records for BTC, ETH, and ADA from Coinbase Pro
(empirical_pipeline/multi_asset_hayashi_yoshida.py).
Because asynchronous order book arrivals induce artifi-
cial Epps attenuation, we estimate the non-synchronous
covariation matrix via the Hayashi and Yoshida[68] esti-
mator, coupled with non-linear Random Matrix Theory
(RMT) eigenvalue shrinkage [70] with shrinkage intensity
α= 0.1512. Inverting the empirical price impacts yields
the high-dimensional cross-impact matrixK ∈R3×3
[33, 50]. As predicted by Theorem M6, multi-asset
cross-coupling inflates the empirical Kreiss constant
fromKdecoupled = 1.4791to Kcoupled = 1.7285, contract-
ing the clearinghouse safe solvency radius by14.43%
(safe radius contracts from$2,028,274.62to$1 ,735,588.01;
results/provenance/multi_asset_hayashi_yoshida_results.json).
This empirical confirmation proves that cross-asset liquid-
ity spillovers significantly exacerbate transient insolvency
exposure, establishing joint pseudospectral monitoring
as an operational necessity.

8.8 Continuous-Time Marked Hawkes
Point Processes and Jump-Diffusion
SDE: Theorem HP1
In high-frequency electronic markets, order arrivals do
not arrive along a metronomic discrete clock: transac-
tions arrive as a mutually exciting point process (Hawkes
process) with power-law clustering and Poisson jump risk
[61].
We extend the discrete-time formulation to a
continuous-time jump-diffusion Stochastic Differential
Equation (SDE):

9 Discussion, Research Frontiers,
and Conclusion
9.1 Intervention under uncertainty and
liquidity feedback
A proposed intervention can reduce order size, slow an
update, retain a previously approved policy, route a deci-
sion to review, or abstain from a specific action. These
are not interchangeable. In particular, freezing learning
need not cancel outstanding orders, and abstaining from
new positions need not remove existing inventory risk.
Proposition 9.1(Common-norm robustness; standard

A probabilistic estimation set yields only a guarantee

9.1.1 Why abstention is not automatically sta-

Any empirical intervention comparison must therefore
record bid–ask spreads, depth, execution failures, inven-
tory exposure, and losses borne by other participants.
An intervention that reduces one agent’s losses by trans-
ferring risk or withdrawing market-making capacity is
not automatically a system improvement. Operational
deployment is outside this manuscript’s scope.
9.1.2 Surveillance considerations, statutory
sandboxes, and legal boundaries
An essential institutional question is whether random-
ized active probing could or should be deployed in live
financial markets by exchanges or regulatory authori-
ties. In live trading, injecting randomized probe orders
without genuine investment intent raises severe statutory
concerns regarding market manipulation and fictitious
transactions, such as under SEC Rule 10b-5 and Sec-
tion 9(a)(2) of the Securities Exchange Act in the U.S.,
or Article 12 of the EU Market Abuse Regulation (MAR).
Active probing is thereforenotproposed as a live market
intervention.
The probing framework has three narrower uses:
1. A lower-bound benchmark.Theorems 3.4–3.6
state what cannot be learned from passive price data
and how much excitation a decisive classification
needs. Like a Cramér–Rao bound, this is informative
without being an operational protocol.
2. Pre-deployment testing.Market-access rules al-
ready require firms to test algorithms before de-
ployment (e.g. MiFID II RTS 6, Article 5; SEC
Rule 15c3-5). Within such non-live test environ-
ments, the probe protocol of section 4 could be used
to estimate a policy’s response. Whether regulators
should mandate it, and whether sandbox responses
transfer to live markets, are open questions; the
transfer failure in section 6 is a caution.
3. Passive reduced-form monitoring.Section 7.4
shows what can be computed from public data with-
out intervention: a reduced-form, model-derived
feedback component with known biases. It cannot
attribute feedback to particular agents, and it does
not certify stability.
Production FIX 5.0 drop-copy supervision
and mandatory abstention fallback.To bridge
theoretical certificates into live banking and ex-
change infrastructure without injecting manipu-
lative active probe orders, we develop an institu-
tional FIX 5.0 SP2 Drop-Copy supervisory controller
(production_runtime/institutional_fix_supervisor.py).
The engine ingests execution drop copies (Tags 11, 35,
50, 54, 38, 44, and disclosed rate parameters in Tag
847) and computes a streaming 10-second Conic Certifi-
cate[ Slower,S upper]in 64.45 microseconds per message.
Operating under a three-state supervisor, the con-
troller permits full agentic trading in the Green state
(Supper < 0.95), throttles aggressive order entry by 50%
in the Yellow state (0.95≤Supper < 1.0), and enforces
an automated Mandatory Abstention Protocol in the
Red state (Supper≥1.0) by dropping aggressive orders,
pegging execution to passive limit orders, and alerting
the human risk desk. Benchmark evaluations on high-
frequency order streams demonstrate zero false-negative
supercritical leakage within the test suite, offering an
operational engineering prototype for pre-trade risk
controls designed around market-access risk principles

against autonomous agentic feedback in electronic
trading.
Agentic Market Invariant Protocol (AMIP RFC-
001) and zero-trust pre-trade interceptor.To
formalize these pre-trade invariants into an open, au-
ditable reference specification for autonomous AI agents
in electronic markets, we specify theAgentic Mar-
ket Invariant Protocol (AMIP RFC-001)and re-
lease the reference inline interceptor agentic_guard
(agentic_guard/core.py). Operating as an inline pre-
trade gateway before order entry into the exchange match-
ing engine, agentic_guard intercepts every raw action
generated by deep reinforcement learning, imitation learn-
ing, or LLM reasoning agents and enforces four sequential
safety barriers:
1. Barrier 1 (Semantic Anti-Hallucination Fil-
ter):Rejects orders with non-positive or non-finite
quantities (q≤0, NaN,∞) or off-market limit prices
deviating by more thanδdev = 3.0%from the pre-
vailing mid-quote, preventing prompt injection or
stochastic generative collapse from corrupting the
book.
2. Barrier 2 (Streaming Conic Feedback Cer-
tification):Evaluates a streaming 2SLS feedback
estimator in sub-25 microseconds to generate the
finite-sample stability interval[Slower,S upper].
3. Barrier 3 (Theorem 3.8 Kreiss Solvency Ra-
dius Enforcement):Dynamically clamps cu-
mulative requested positions to |It + ∆ It| ≤
r∗
i = Bi/(MC·Pt)(or provably conservatively
r∗
i,conservative = Bi/(αnK(J)·Pt)with αn ≤en,
bounded above by Bi/(K(J)Pt)), where MC =
supt∥CJt∥2 is the directly evaluated peak transient

4. Barrier 4 (Three-State Fallback State Ma-
chine):Enforces Green ( Supper < 0.95, unre-
stricted), Yellow (0.95≤Supper < 1.0, 50% order
throttle), and Red (Supper ≥1.0, Mandatory Ab-
stention). Under Red state, aggressive liquidity-
taking orders (Market/IOC) are strictly halted (0%
leakage), while liquidity-providing limit orders are
converted to passive pegged orders with a 75% size
reduction.
In a benchmark 10-agent financial society simulation
(agentic_guard/multi_agent_sandbox.py) subjected
to simultaneous prompt hallucinations and momen-
tum runaway shocks, the unprotected market suf-
fered a−31.22%flash crash with multiple bankrupt-
cies. Under agentic_guard, maximum drawdown was
constrained to−0.85%(a36 .7×risk reduction) with
zero agent defaults and a mean interception latency
of52 .81 µs, well within the sub-100 µsinstitutional
HFT requirement. The core invariant is formally mech-
anized in Lean 4 (Proofs/AgenticGuard.lean, Theo-
remagentic_guard_composite_safety).
Real Level-3 order book replay and multi-provider
LLM tool gateway.To evaluate agentic_guard
against live market depth rather than stylized agent
interactions, we implement an institutional gate-
way (agentic_guard/institutional_gateway.py and
real_lob_benchmark.py) that natively ingests function-
calling tool payloads across OpenAI, Anthropic Claude,
and Google Gemini schemas. Replaying genuine histor-
ical Level-3 depth snapshots fromBTC_1sec.csv (500
ticks,5,000total orders) with adversarial prompt injec-

supercritical momentum cascades), the unprotected mar-
ket experienced a−350.72%drawdown with8out of10
autonomous agents defaulting (80.0%bankruptcy rate,
95%Clopper–Pearson CI[44.4%, 97.5%]). In contrast,
the AMIP Institutional Gateway prevented simulated
agent bankruptcies in this sample (0.0%default rate in
sample,95%CI[0 .0%, 30.8%]) and constrained draw-
down to−16.59%(a21 .1×risk reduction), achieving an
average pre-trade inspection latency of3.44µs(99th per-
centile9.12 µs) with cryptographic SHA-256 provenance
hashing structured to support pre-trade risk auditabil-
ity aligned with SEC Rule 15c3-5 and MiFID II RTS 6
recordkeeping objectives.
9.2 Executed numerical checks and em-
pirical ledgers
9.2.1 Purpose and reproducibility
The computations reported here test agreement between
formulas and direct matrix calculations. They neither
fit the model to a financial dataset nor train finan-
cial agents. Section B contains the exact self-contained
Python/NumPy program used for the reported matrix
checks. The run used NumPy 2.3.5, double-precision
arrays, and seed 20260918 for the network samples. Last-
digit differences across numerical libraries are immaterial
to the stated tolerances.

direct eigenvalue calculations is approximately2.22×
10−16.

nonnegative pairs withd = 3market coordinates and

9.2.3 Prospective experimental protocols and
validation frontiers
To delineate established scientific findings from open
research frontiers, the prospective multi-agent reinforce-
ment learning (MARL) protocol (specifying hypotheses
H1–H5, double-auction exchange simulation, and actor-
critic agent families) and the prospective institutional
validation protocol (specifying decision provenance, au-
thorized staggered rollouts, and shadow evaluation) are
formalized in Sections S14 and S15 of the accompanying
Mathematical Supplement.
9.3 Funded dealers under genuine feed-
back: what drives halts
Withdrawn simulation results.Earlier versions con-
tained four simulation results in this section, and all four
are withdrawn:
• A “solvency phase diagram”.The price process
in that simulation was an exogenous AR(1). The
feedback parameterS never entered the price dy-
namics. The “non-AI” benchmark was random order
noise, not the inventory-control rule described.
• A cross-simulator coverage table.Its classifica-
tion rule used a hand-calibrated growth-rate thresh-
old, not the certificate of section 4. Its simulated
prices were integrated, soS = 1was not their stabil-
ity boundary.
• A decisiveness frontier.Its “proposed method”
was the same growth-rate heuristic.
• DQN and continuous actor-critic action-
support comparisons.The DQN updated only its
output layer, and the actor-critic was never trained.
The tabularQ-learning transfer study of section 6 does
not depend on these simulations and is unaffected. This
subsection replaces the phase diagram with a simulation
in whichSactually governs the price.
Design.We use the single-cohort case of (29). Price
deviation and adaptive pressure follow
xt+1 =ax t +yt +λnt +σet, y t+1 = (1−η)yt +ηQxt,
withS =a+Q. Adaptive demand isDt =yt/λandnt is
i.i.d. noise flow. A dealer takes the other side of all flow
ft =Dt +nt +ϕIt. The termϕIt is the offsetting flow
its quote skew attracts (inventory control in the spirit
of 4). The dealer earns a half-spread on|ft|and marks
inventoryIt to pt = 1 +xt. It halts when|It|pt >LE t
or Et≤0. We compare the feedback market with three
markets that havenoprice-to-flow loop, in which flow is
exogenous and its price impact is retained:
•B1: i.i.d. flow with the same variance;
• B2: AR(1) flow with the same variance and first
autocorrelation;
• B3: AR(1) flow with the same variance and long-run
variance.
Each cell uses 400 seeds over 500 steps, and param-
eters are illustrative (a = 0.5, η= 0.2, λ= 0.002;
simulations/feedback_dealer.py).
Proposition 9.2(Zero-frequency gain of feedback flow).
ForS <1, the long-run variance offt =Dt +nt (without

From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
inventory control) is
LRV(f) = (1−a)2σ2

Proof. At frequency zero the recursions give(1−a)x =

and f =y/λ+n =n(1−a)/(1−S) +Qσe/(λ(1−S)).
The long-run variance is the squared zero-frequency gain
applied to the innovation variances.
Results.Table 34 reports the halt rates.
• Halt rates rise steeply withS well inside the stable
region. AtL = 10and ϕ= 0.05they go from 11%
atS= 0.5to 93% atS= 0.8.
• No-loop flow matched on variance produces 15% at
S = 0.8(B1), and matching the first autocorrelation
as well produces 22% (B2).
• No-loop flow matched on long-run variance produces
98% (B3), as many halts as the feedback market or
more.
In this model, then, dealer failure is governed by the
low-frequency content of the flow the dealer absorbs.
Feedback matters because, by theorem 9.2, it inflates
that content by the factor(1−S)−2as S→1. Two
consequences follow:
• The earlier claim that this vulnerability is “absent
in non-AI market makers” is not supported. Any
source of equally persistent flow produces it.
• A benchmark that matches only volatility, or only
short-lag autocorrelation, understates the risk.
This is the operational content of the claim that local
price stability does not ensure dealer safety: the stable
region contains values ofS at which a capital-constrained
dealer fails with high probability. The simulation is linear,
has one dealer with fixed capital and no strategic with-
drawal, and uses uncalibrated parameters. It illustrates
a mechanism and does not estimate an event probability.
9.4 Limit Order Book FIFO Queue Pri-
ority and Fill Dynamics
Barrier 4 of the AMIP RFC-001 specification mandates
that throttled aggressive market orders be converted
into passive pegged limit orders at the touch (25%
size). However, on institutional electronic exchanges (e.g.,
NASDAQ, CME, Coinbase), matching engines enforce
First-In-First-Out (FIFO) Price-Time Priority. A newly
pegged limit order joins thebackof the touch queue,
facing queue depletion wait time and adverse selection
risk [63].
To evaluate queue-priority microstructural realities,
we implement a tick-level FIFO matching simulator on
Level-3 Bitcoin order book records from Coinbase Pro
(agentic_guard/hft_queue_priority_model.py). An
AMIP pegged order is placed at the tail of the prevailing
inside depthQahead = bids_notional_0. The order exe-
cutes only after incoming aggressive market orders and
cancellations depleteQahead.
We benchmark two execution policies across empirical
trade events:
1. Naive Static Pegged AMIP:Orders are stat-
ically pegged at25%volume regardless of queue
depth. While achieving an88.5%fill rate (average
time-to-fill4.56s), the dealer incurs substantial ad-
verse selection when filled by toxic momentum runs,
suffering$559.65in cumulative toxic selection losses.
2. Queue-Aware Dynamic AMIP with Inventory
Dampening:Order sizes are dynamically scaled by
prevailing queue congestion and Avellaneda-Stoikov
inventory penalization:
u∗
pegged =θ·u0·max
(
0.10,1− Qahead
Qahead +Q depth
)
·exp(−γ·qt·sgn(u)).
(92)
This dynamic queue-aware policy preserves
the high88 .5%fill rate while compressing
toxic adverse selection losses to$283 .35(a
49.37%reduction in toxic losses) and stabi-
lizing dealer inventory variance by77 .68%
(results/provenance/hft_queue_priority_results.json).
This demonstrates that incorporating microstructural
FIFO queue awareness prevents dealers from accumulat-
ing toxic inventory at the tail of congested order books.
9.5 Hardware-Enforced Streaming Inter-
vention: FPGA RTL and SIMD La-
tency Bounds
A critical requirement for institutional deployment in
electronic trading is deterministic, sub-microsecond la-
tency. If a supervisory risk gateway adds more than a
few microseconds of latency, it introduces severe adverse
selection and queue-priority decay, rendering it unusable
for high-frequency trading (HFT) matching engines.
To resolve this frontier, we specify and benchmark
three complementary hardware implementations of the
AMIP RFC-001 pre-trade gating protocol:
1. FPGA Register-Transfer-Level (RTL)
Pipeline:We implement a fully

(agentic_guard/hardware/amip_fpga_pipeline.v)
targeting a Xilinx UltraScale+ FPGA (AMD Alveo
U50) operating at400 MHz (2.5 ns clock period).
The pipeline comprises four stages: (Stage 1) Order
unpacking and account state hash lookup; (Stage 2)
Fixed-point signed inventory and rate calculation;
(Stage 3) Kreiss safe-radius boundary comparator

9.6 Phantom Depth, Quote Stuffing, and
Anti-Spoofing Interceptors: Theo-
rem SP1
A sophisticated adversarial HFT exploit against pre-trade
risk filters isquote stuffingandphantom depth generation.
Because the dynamic Kreiss safe radius scales with order
book depthr∗∝B/(K(J)·P), an attacker can submit
thousands of fleeting passive limit orders far from mid
to artificially bloat measured depthVt. This tricks naive
pre-trade filters into wideningr∗, after which the attacker
executes an aggressive market sweep before cancelling
their passive quotes.
To eliminate this vulnerability, AMIP incorporates
Barrier 1b (Cancellation-Discounted Effective
Depth):

+ (1−ˆχk,t)·I(OTRk≤10)

where ˆχk,t∈[0, 1)is the streaming cancellation rate and
OTRk is the order-to-trade ratio of cohortk.
Theorem 9.3(Anti-Spoofing Invariance (Theorem
SP1)).Let an adversary inject an arbitrary phantom
depth multiplierM·V0 with cancellation rateχadv = 1−ϵ.
AsM→∞withϵ→0,
1. The quadratic OTR penalty bounds effective depth:
limϵ→0MV0·ϵ2≤const≪V0.

9.7 Physical Wire-to-Wire Hardware La-
tency Stack and Sub-350ns Cut-
Through
To establish the physical real-world viability of AMIP
in premier colocation environments, we develop a cycle-
accurate hardware timing model of a complete dual-port
100GbE QSFP28 FPGA bump-in-the-wire risk appliance
(agentic_guard/hardware/wire_to_wire_latency_model.py).
The physical stack accounts for:
1. Optical Propagation & Transceivers:1-meter
OM4 multimode optical fiber (5 .0 ns) coupled
to 100GBASE-SR4 QSFP28 transceivers (15.0 ns
optical-to-electrical conversion).
2. 100G Ethernet MAC/PCS Layer:Xilinx Ultra-
Scale+ Integrated 100G Ethernet Subsystem oper-
ating in RS-FEC bypass cut-through mode (85.0 ns
PMA/PCS deserialization +25 .0 ns cut-through
MAC).
3. Pipelined FIX/ITCH Protocol Parser:3-cycle
streaming parser at400.0MHz (7.5ns).
4. AMIP Pre-Trade Core:8-cycle deterministic
Verilog risk pipeline (20.0ns).
5. Egress Packetization & Serializer:Clock
domain crossing (5.0 ns), TX MAC (25 .0 ns),
TX PMA/PCS (85.0 ns), and optical transmitter
(15.0ns).
The nominal wire-to-wire cut-through latency is292.5 ns,
and with a conservative47.5 ns clock jitter margin, the
99thpercentile latency is340.0ns, providing a+160.0 ns
timing margin below the institutional500ns HFT SLA
(results/provenance/wire_to_wire_latency_report.json).
9.8 Explicit Methodological Exclusions
and Scope Omissions
This subsection lists what the models and evidence do
not cover.
Mathematical and Proof Exclusions.The core
positive-feedback boundary proofs (Theorem 2.5) assume
a single persistence scalar and a common learning rate.
We deliberately abstract away from asset-specific persis-
tence, asymmetric learning rates, delayed observations,
and signed response matrices. The non-identifiability
results are derived for discrete-time linear models and
have not been extended to nonlinear or continuous-time
jump-diffusion price processes. The deterministic norm
inequalities (Proposition S8.1) do not address the statis-
tical problems associated with continuous model updates,
adaptive sampling, and post-selection confidence in highly
non-linear settings. Finally, while the exact-arithmetic
outer bounds are theoretically proven (Theorem 4.2), the
numerical floating-point conic implementations are main-
tained as unverified candidates because their complete
outward constraint assembly cannot be formally certified
without infinite precision.
Market Dynamics and Economic Scope.Our base-
line market model isolates the specific friction of algo-
rithmic feedback. The spectral boundaries derived herein

57

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 58 ===
From Human Markets to Machine Markets Research Manuscript
represent local mechanism tests, not universal asset-
pricing theorems or global crisis predictors. While styl-
ized multi-clearinghouse margin networks are formalized
under Eisenberg–Noe clearing (Theorem CC1), our mod-
els explicitly exclude un-cleared bilateral over-the-counter
(OTC) derivative unwind cascades, cross-currency collat-
eral haircuts, and macroeconomic fiscal feedback loops.
The primary financial simulation isolates excess-demand
mechanics, while our dedicated microstructure extensions
evaluate empirical order book ladders and queue priori-
ties.
Algorithmic Architectures and Empirical Surveil-
lance.The analytical framework focuses on linear adap-
tive memory and its tractable nonlinear envelopes. While
continuous-action Deep MARL co-adaptation is inves-
tigated in Section 6.7, we deliberately abstract from
foundation-model LLM agent swarms with unconstrained
natural-language planning. Crucially, our active random-
ized probing protocol assumes normative agent reactions;
we do not model adversarial scenarios where sophisti-
cated participants detect surveillance probes and actively
distort their quoting behavior to obfuscate their feed-
back loops. The empirical analysis combines a 12-day
high-resolution Level-3 baseline with a systematic 768
asset-day panel on Binance Spot, cross-venue replica-
tion, and the FI-2010 benchmark; however, the estimates
remain reduced-form and lack counterparty identifiers
(ContractI1), and acquiring continuous multi-year raw
PCAP packet archives of sub-millisecond Level-3 book
state across all national market system venues remains
an ongoing institutional infrastructure task for market
surveillance authorities.
9.9 Conclusion
We asked what market data can reveal about feedback
between adaptive trading and prices.
The paper has three findings.
• Passive price data have exact limits.Aggre-
gate price responses identify only merged rate–weight
pairs (Theorem 3.4). Separating near-coincident co-
horts needs a probe budget growing likeδ−4(The-
orem 3.5). Any error-controlled stability classifier
must abstain nearS = 1, and with finite windows it
must do so at every budget (Theorem 3.6).
• Local price stability does not imply dealer
safety.The safe shock radius is governed by
supt∥CJt∥, which the Kreiss constant bounds and
the spectral radius does not (Theorem 3.8). In
a funded simulation with genuine feedback, dealer
halts rise steeply inside the stable region because
feedback inflates the low-frequency content of flow
by(1 −S)−2(theorem 9.2). Non-adaptive flow with
the same persistence does the same.
• The public data are consistent with weak
momentum feedback.Across twelve days on
Coinbase Pro, an eleven-day cross-venue replica-
tion, and a 768 asset-day panel on Binance Spot
(2021–2024), estimated feedback is consistently small
and positive ( ˆQ≈0.008, ˆS≈0.92 < 1), cor-
roborated by structural minimum-distance estima-
tion (QSMM = 0.0086), while Rigobon heteroskedas-
ticity identifies contemporaneous shock buffering
(QRig =−0.0191). It is not causal and is not at-
tributable to algorithms.
An earlier version of this paper reported instrumental-
variable estimates, panel regressions and simulation re-
sults that, on audit, were not produced from data or did
not implement the stated model. They are withdrawn,
and Appendix D maps every remaining number to the
code that produces it. The main open problems are a
credible source of exogenous variation in live markets,
counterparty-level data for attribution, and a solvency
analysis with strategic liquidity withdrawal.
Data, code, and replication avail-
ability
All code, tests and generated result files are in the release
repository. Tables marked as generated are written
by scripts/build_results.py from JSON outputs
in results/provenance/, and Appendix D lists every
empirical and simulated number with its producing
script. The empirical analysis in section 7.4 uses the files
empirical_pipeline/data/{BTC,ETH,ADA}_1sec.csv,
sourced from Nielsen [58] (Kaggle dataset
1567989, CC0 Public Domain, recorded from
Coinbase Pro via crypto-rl by Sadighian).
Complete file checksums, schema definitions,
and timing validations are documented in
empirical_pipeline/data/README.md and verified
in tests/test_data_provenance_and_closure.py.
Sections 7.2 and 7.3 use cached Binance US snap-
shots and public Binance kline archives stored under
research_extension/; section 7’s Coinbase sam-
ples are cached in the same tree. The regulated
equity benchmark in section 7.4 uses the canoni-
cal FI-2010 limit order book dataset [ 59] stored at
empirical_pipeline/fi2010/data/data.csv (CC0
1.0 Universal public domain). Synthetic cross-venue
latency instruments from earlier preliminary drafts are
not used and are completely withdrawn.

A Proofs and technical qualifica-
tions
A.1 Local stability and the limits of lin-
earization
For the stable part of Proposition 2.3, choose γ∈
(ρ(J∗), 1). Finite-dimensional matrix theory provides
an equivalent vector norm with∥J∗∥< γ. Continuity
of DΦprovides a convex neighborhood ofz∗and ˜γ <1
such that∥DΦ(z)∥≤˜γthroughout that neighborhood.
The mean-value integral yields
∥Φ(z)−z∗∥≤˜γ∥z−z∗∥.(95)
A sufficiently small norm ball is invariant; iteration gives
exponential decay. The unstable part is the standard
discrete-time linearization instability result: a derivative
eigenvalue outside the closed unit disk produces a locally
expanding direction and rules out Lyapunov stability
for a continuously differentiable map. No conclusion
is asserted from an eigenvalue on the unit circle alone.
The result concerns the fixed autonomous map and its
fixed point, not an arbitrary sequence of re-estimated
Jacobians.
A.2 Proof of the positive-feedback
boundary
Letν=ρ(BC)and define
r+ = a+ 1−η+
√
(a−1 +η)2 + 4gην
2 .(96)
When gν >0, Perron–Frobenius theory suppliesv≥0,
v̸= 0, withBCv=νv. ThenCv̸= 0and
w= η
r+−1 +ηCv(97)
satisfies J+(v,w )⊤= r+(v,w )⊤. Hence r+ is an eigen-
value.
To show that no eigenvalue has greater modulus, let
(v,w )be a nonzero eigenvector with eigenvalueλ. Eigen-
values equal toa or1 −ηhave modulus at mostr+. For
the others, eliminatingwgives
BCv= (λ−a)(λ−1 +η)
gη v.(98)
If|λ|>r+, then|λ|>max(a,1−η)and
|(λ−a)(λ−1 +η)|≥(|λ|−a)(|λ|−(1−η))(99)
>(r +−a)(r+−(1−η)) =gην.
(100)
This contradicts the spectral-radius bound on eigenvalues
of BC. Thusρ(J+) =r+. Ifg = 0, the matrix is block
triangular. If ν= 0and g > 0, the same elimination
excludeseveryeigenvalueoutside {a,1−η}, sotheformula
reduces tomax(a,1−η)and remains valid.
Finally,r + <1is equivalent to
√
(a−1 +η)2 + 4gην <1−a+η.(101)
The right side is positive. Squaring and simplifying yields
gν <1−abecause η >0. Equality and strict reversal
follow identically. For a finite-dimensional constant linear
system, all eigenvalues inside the unit disk are equivalent
to asymptotic stability. This proves theorem 2.5.
A.3 What the boundary does not imply
A positive real eigenvalue passing through one identi-
fies loss of local linear stability in the specified model.
It does not determine the nonlinear attractor, whether
prices crash or grow, whether an institution defaults, or
whether a finite disturbance remains inside the modeled
region. A nonlinear extension could saturate or switch
into another regime. An observed financial crisis can
also occur without this crossing, for example when an
external shock overwhelms solvency buffers.
The result uses a single persistence scalara and a com-
monη. Allowing asset-specific persistence, asymmetric
learningrates, delayedobservation, orsignedresponsema-
trices removes the simple formula unless further structure
is proved. A claimed universal surface in six aggregate
variables would need to show that the omitted state is
either irrelevant, bounded, or measured. The current
theorem does not establish such sufficiency.
A.4 Stochastic and nonlinear robustness
For a nonlinear mapΦt with a common reference point,
suppose the inducedP-norm of every derivative on a
convex ball of radiusr is at most γ <1. Then the
same Lipschitz argument yields the disturbance bound
in equation (90). If¯b≤(1−γ)r and the initial state is
inside the ball, that ball is invariant. With an evolving
reference point, its motion must appear as an additional
disturbance; ignoring that drift changes the claim.
If an estimated uncertainty set covers the actual ma-
trices with probability at least1−δover the relevant
horizon, the robust bound holds on that coverage event.
Marginal per-time coverage does not automatically imply
simultaneous horizon coverage. Model updates, adap-
tive sampling, and post-selection can invalidate a naive
confidence statement. These statistical issues are work
remaining, not solved by the deterministic norm inequal-
ity.

Identifiability across the parameter space is partitioned

1. Case 1 (Knownη, full-rankU):When the com-

singleton rate interval also identifiesq. An unknown
nondegenerate rate interval does not identify the
rate whenq= 0.
5. Case 5 (Possible nullspace ambiguity):With
known rate and a feasibleq0, the nonnegative iden-
tified set is(q0 + kerU )∩Rn
+. At a strictly positive
